RUGBY.

INTERMEDIATES GOING AHEAD.

Old Boys' Eager Enthusiasm to Join the New League.

WHAT IS BEING DONE. BY "SCRUM." The present week has been one of hustle in , regard to the formation of the Intermediate Northern Rugby League. I hear from all quar- ters of the enthusiasm which prevails on the t part of the Old Boys, and this is a happy sign. If the interest is maintained, as I fully expect it will be, there is no reason to doubt that by next month, the new League will be well on the • way to having a successful season:

I „ 1 •

Mr. E. Jackson Mee, the secretary pro tern., has to report an excellent week's work, and he writes as follows The interest shown and the enthusiasm dis- played during the week in the new League has exceeded the hopes of its most ardent supporters. Several meetings have been held which have been attended by eager, enthusiastic lads who

. . r t

. really want to participate in the healthy, vigor- ous game and to uphold the honour of their -old school. r" St. James' and Beamont Old Boys unani- mously decided to organise two teams, and this means 26 boys will regularly play the game. Silver-street will no 'doubt adopt the same plan if practice matches reveal sufficient talent.

Club secretaries should make early applica- tion for admission to the League. Signing-on forms will be sent out next week to secretaries who apply for thein. Club colours shoun also be registered early, as certain colours are very :..-opalar—(Who said "Primrose and Blue?")-1 the committee may decide the question on the policy of first come first served. To all boys who want to play Rugby, the fol- lowing advice is given : Old Boys should make inquiries at their old school to see if an Old Boys' team is to be formed. If no team is formed then write to 11, Enville-street. Remember the only qualification to Warring- ton boys is that all players must be under 17 on the first day of• September. Budding referees are reminded to come for- ward to what will prove a splendid training ground.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. JAMES' OLD BOYS' R.F.C.

The above club was formed at an enthusiastic meeting held on Monday evening. It was pleas- ing to note the presence of several gentlemen who are keenly interested in the organisation of the club. Forty lads who are eligible to play in the Intermediate Rugby League were also present. Mr. C. George presided and explained the object of holding the meeting. After coming to a decision to join the League, a long discussion followed as to the advisability of entering two teams. It was pointed out that one strong team might be raised, whereas running two teams might mean both being weak, but the lads proved good sportsmen and decided to enter two teams in the League in order to give an opportunity for most of the members to play the game. The following officers were appointed: Presi- dent, Mr. W. H. Stansfield; Vice-presidents, the Managers; secretary, Mr. C. George; treasurer, Mr. C. Holt; assistant sec. and trees., Mr. Duncan Mann; committee, Messrs. E. Jack- son Mee, A. Todd; A. Holbrook, J. Bibby, A. Higham, S. Rigby, antfthe two captains. Thirty- seven boys gave their names an daddresses for forms of membership.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====

At a meeting of Old Boys of the above school, heht on Monday evening, Mr. E. H. Turton, headmaster, was in the chair. It was unani- mously decided to form an Old Boys' Club and join the Warrington and District N.U.R. Inter- mediate League. The following were elected members of the committee: Messrs. E. H. Tnrton (chairman), J. Whitehouse. J. Cooper, T. Warren, A. Jones (captain), C. Edwards (vice-captain), and E. A. Cannell (hon. see. and treas.).

BEAMONT OLD BOYS.

At the Beamont Council School most enthusi- astic scenes were witnessed, over 60 Old Boys turning up. It was unanimously decided to run two teams in connection with the school.

Mr. Mason. (headmaster) was elected president of the Beamont organisation. and Mr. James treasurer and representative of the clubs on the League Football Committee. Both clubs elected their secretaries and com- mitteemen, and are keenly waiting to become registered players and start work in earnest.

IN THE COURTS.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
"MEAN, CONTEMPTIBLE FRAUD." Magistrate and Prisoner's Conduct.

"You will go to prison for a month, and you have got off very reasonably. It is a mean, contemptible fraud." So spoke Dr. Joseph, the chairman of the Warrington Bench of magistrates at the Folice Court on Monday, to James Conroy, no fixed abode, charged with obtaining board and lodg- ing from Sarah Atkinson, a 'widow, Uf 43, Orford-street, to the extent of 165., with the object of defrauding her. The Prisoner: I would go to the Liverpool Assizes for trial. The Chief Constable said that he would have to ask for an adjournment to give notice to the Clerk and provide additional particulars. On Monday, Bth June, Conroy went to the house of the widow at 43, Orford-street, and represented himself to be a club collector. He lodged there for over a week and ran up a bill for foal and lodging, amounting to 16s. He pretended to go out to carry on his work daily, and took home books to show that he was working. Later he absconded and was arrested by Deteptive-Sergeant Reddington on a warrant. Dr. Joseph: Do you know that if you want to go for trial you will be kept in custody till the next Sessions six weeks hence? _

..„.„. Prisoner: Try me now, then. I plead guilty to this case The Chief Constable said prisoner alluded to other cases. He had gone to other places and dbtained sums of money by false pretences. He had obtained ss. from a poor woman, represent- ing that he was a club collector, but so far as Warrington was concerned there would be no prosecutions in those cases. Accused's brother was a collector and a highly respected man. He had helped accused, but Conroy had refused to stick to the work, and had thus brought trouble upon himself and his friends. The Bench sent him to prison month.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
BOROUGH POLICE COURT

TUESDAY. Befoi s Counrillor H. Roberts (in the chair), and X aw's• J. RY/Iler Young and C. Jackson Holnee.

Ten Months' Married Life.—A young couple sa-ge been separated after ten months of mar- ried life. Amy Gleave, 53, Chorley-street, son&lit separation from Janies Henry Gleave, 17, Clare-street. She said that she was mar- ried ten months ago, and her husband had been idle about five months of the time. She had to pawn things from the house to get food. In June, on the advice of Mr. Laws, the Police Court Missionary, she went into the Workhouse, where a child was born. Her husband had struck and threatened her.—Mr. Laws said that lie had tried to see the husband, but he had always kept out of his way until the wife went into the Workhouse, and then he came and scolded him for advising her to go.—The wife was granted a separation order and allowed Bs. per week and the custody. of the child.

THURSDAY. Before Mr. A. J. Jolley (in the chair), Dr. Naden, Messrs. W. Roberts, and W. T. Wil- liams. Drunk in Charge of Child.—Catherine Far- rington, 12, Dolman's-lane, was charged with having been drunk while in charge of a child. She pleaded guilty.—P.C. Harrison said on Tues- day he saw the woman staggering about Dol- man's-lane. She fell and the child, which had been in her arms, fell below her. He locked her up.—lnspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., asked that the case might be adjourned a month , so that he could keep a watch upon the woman. —The Chief Constable said that, if possible, they wished to reclaim her. The case was ad- journed for a month.

A Card of Thanks.

One subscriber to a paper in a certain town, not to be outdone in the matter of thanks, sent in the following to the editor: "Mr. Editor.—l desire to thank the friends and neighbours most heartily in this manner for their co-operation during the illness and death of my late hus- band, who escaped from me by the hand of death last Friday while eating breakfast. To all my friends and all who contributed so sv illing to make the last moments and the funeral a success I desire to remember most kindly, hop- ing these lines will find them enjoying the same blessing. I have also a good milch cow and a roan gelding horse which I will sell cheap. 'God moves in a mysterious way His wonder to perform. He plants His footsteps on the sea and ride 9 upon the storm.' Also a black and white shoat cheap."

===== 0002601_19140718_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 18, 1914.

NEW ST. BENEDICT'S.

Lord Abbot Lays the Foundation Stone.

The breeding pens should now be dispersed, since hatching is not recommended during July. As the pens are being dissolved a rigid selection should be made, and all two-year-old birds dis- posed of. It is a mistake to retain ordinary laying -stock after it is two years old, since in the majority of cases the tens do not pay for the food they consume or the space they occupy. Several authoritative experiments have proved that a hen is in her prime during her first and second seasons, after which time the supply of eggs rapidly decreases until she ceases to be profitable. Highly-bred exhibition stock is not, of course, included in this category, for in many cases it pays to retain a hen in the breeding pen ior several years, even though she produce but a dozen eggs in the season, But with ordinary laying stock ,it pays better to rely on young birds, and to dispose of those who are more than two years old. The present is the best time in which to sell old hens immediately before they go into the moult. It pays better to sell them at this time rather than keep them while they are changing their feathers, and then, perhaps, ob- tain 6d. or 9d. more. A bird hatched during February, March, or April does not go into the moult until a year the following June, as the period she is getting her adult feathers cannot rightly be termed a moult. Thus, when she first commences to lose her feathers she is fiffeen months old, and just before she enters her second moult she should be sold, when she is, roughly speaking, twenty-seven months of age.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
A PICTURESQUE SPECTACLE.

Thousands of Catholics in Warrington re- I joiced on Sunday, when the foundation-stone of a new church was laid by the Right Rev. the I Lord Abbot of Amplefortlf. The church, which is to be dedicated to St. Benedict, will adjoin the presbytery in Orford- lane, and will be built in the Byzantine style to accommodate about 600 worshippers, whilst the cost is estimated to reach .C 6,000. The new building will be connected with the presbytery, which was erected several years ago, and will consist of a nave, 70 feet long and 40 feet wide, with passage aisles; a large sanctuary, which will be seen from all parts of the church; a lady chapel, and a chapel of the Blessed Sacrament. In addition there are to be three confessionals (two on one side and one on the other), a bap- tistry, choir gallery, narthex, two porches, a priest's sacristy, boys' sacristy, and working sacristy, whilst under the latter the heating chamber and storage rooms will be built. The vaulting and panelled ceilings are to be finished in white plaster, but it is expected that an elaborate colour scheme will be undertaken in the near future. It is expected that the church will be erected in about a year. The contractor is Mr. James Pilkington, of Rainford; the clerk of works, Mr. Pierpoint; and the architect, Mr. Matthew llonan, of Liverpool, who will also be responsible for the new presbytery and the re-modelling at the St. Benedict's Schools.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
SERVICE AND PROCESSION.

A short service was held in the old church on Sunday afternoon, after which a procession was formed, which wended its way along Clegg-street and Orford-lane towards the new site. Large crowds lined the streets, and there were many manifestations of the respect in which the church and its officers are held by the adherents of the Roman Catholic faith. About a thou- sand took part in the procession, which had banners _here and there.

The following was the order of the procession: Cross-bearers and acolytes. Altar boys. League of the Cross Band. St. Mary's Catholic Young Men's Society. St. Mitchel's (Ditton) Catholic Young Men's Society. St. Alban's Catholic Young Men's Society. Children of Mary. The Right Rev. the Lord Abbot of Ample- forth and his attendant clergy, together with the Mayor and Mayoress (Councillor and Mrs. Peacock).

By the time the Abbot, in his robes, reached ground the enclosure was densely packed, -tilst the crowd outside strived to obtain points o varl-sge. Shortly after the service • had corn- menpe,l the rain began to fall and continued without !.essation until near the conclusion.

_ Ttur, clergy taking part in the service, in addition to the Abbot, were the Revs. Fathers bwarbreck, Jackson, Wilson (Liverpool), Wilson (St. Mary's, Warrington), Lane-Fox, Whittle, Primavesi, P. Swarbreck, Walmsle,y, Mawson, and Tallon.

During the service the Abbot left the plat- form to traverse the excavations, where the foundations are to be erected. The ceremony was choral, and in addition to the accompani- ments by the band, Mr. W. Turner, the St. Benedict's organist, played a harmonium. The choir boys from St. Benedict's, St. Mary's, and St. Alban's—the three Benedictine churches in the town—formed a massed choir, under the baton of Mr. Eckersley, the organist and choir- master at St. Mary's.

THE ABBOT'S ADDRESS. At the conclusion. the Right Rev. the Lord Abbot, addressing the assembly, said : • I have now to tell you brethren—members of this congregation and other congregations, and also those who do not belong to our Church— that we have completed the work of laving the foundation-stone of the new Church of St. Bene- dict, and that this is a very poor congregation. God will bless any offerings you might Tut upon it. Those who are able to get to the stone may, if they wish, put their offerings on the stone, and those who are not able will find an oppor- tunity on the ground to give their offerings to those carrying the boxes. As we repeated just now in Latin, we will repeat in English "Bless, 0 Lord, this creature of stone, and grant by the invocation of Thy Holy Name that, whosoever, with a pure mind, shall give aid to the building of this church may rive health of body and healing of mind. Through Christ our Lord." He asked them to sing the Hymn of St. Bene- dict. Following this and the singing of "Faith of our Fathers," the Abbot pronounced the Solemn Benediction.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY MORNING SERVICE.

..... _ --- There was a large congregation at the service at the old church on Sunday Morning, when the Abbott based his discourse upon the im- portance of self-sacrifice. He said they were about to build a churl which was to be the home of the Blessed Sacrament. That structure would he dedicated to St. Benedict, a saint who, with their Lord, had a common virtue--that of self-sacrifice. If the congregation wished to see the completion' of the church they would have to practise that virtue. This distinguishing character of their Lord during His life on earth was His spirit of self- sacrifice, for He left the Court of Heaven to come to them. He left the Divine society of the Father to live upon earth; and He did so to sacrifice Himself for them. If there was one saint in the history of Christendom who had followed their Lord's example it was St. Bene- dict, who was born of high parentage, but left everything that belonged to him and fled into the desert in order that he might serve the good God. He lived by himself and subsisted upon what a neighbouring monk used to bring him from his own dinner. He led the life of a hermit until lie was called to his great work— the foundation of his great Order. It would be by. ,self-sacrifice that the new church would be built, maintained, and beauti- fied; indeed if they looked through the history of mankind they would find that nothing great had been done on earth without it. If a great thing was to be done it, was clone by one man or by .a-nation, and, great truths were told by men and women whO made sacrifices. That was so in all kinds of life, in the religious and in the secular. If a business prospered it did so on someone's great sacrifice. So in the Church, and if it was lacking, failure was the result.

Concluding, the Abbott said he acknowledged the self-sacrifice of that congregation in the past, for in spite of the great difficulties they had stood by that poor church and honoured God in it, so much so that they had supported their priests and made it possible for them to build another church. St. Benedict built 12 churches, Without any means at his command by the constant work of his own life. He traiAed simple country folk with no art. He trained them to become skilled huil4ers, and left behind churches that are last- .ing still to the honour of God, and the glory of His Church. They had stood the test of time for centuries, and would remain so till the end.

NORTHERN ONTARIO GOLD DISTRICTS.

The "Financial News (London, July Bth) says that Sir Lomer Couin, Prime Minister of Quebec, has just paid a visit to Northern On- tario and inapected some of the Kirkland Lake and other gold properties. It quotes him as saying that in his judgment this part of Canada will eventually furnish the world's gold supply. His statement is : "I believe the Kirkland mining industry is still in its infancy, although there have been great tlevelopments. The mines are the richest I have ever heard of, and the opportunities for the future are extraordinarily encouraging. I believe that this part of the country will be the gold supply for the entire world."

FARM AND GARDEN.

A FAVOURABLE OUTLOOK.

The "Times" report of the condit.i.o.n of the crops on July Ist is satisfactory. For the pur- poses of the report the number 100 is taken to represent perfect healthiness with average growth and development for the time of the year, a. standard of perfection that is seldom reached over a county much less over the entir3 country.

The fact is that ten years' averages (Which are very constant) vary only slightly around 90 per cent. of a full crop, while the annual aver- ages over Great Britain seldom touch 100 per cent. Only on one occasion during the last 20 years has the general wheat average exceeded 100—namely, 103.5 in October, 1898—although they have on several occasions worked out to 97, 98, and 99 per cent, over the entire area.

In spite of many untoward events, the crops on July 1, 1914, bade fair to maintain the aver- age of the preceding ten years and cereals and beans are at least average crops. Wheat is in- deed considerably above the 10 years' average and six points above last year's estimate at the same date.

The standards attained this year are as • follows :

Wheat, 95.1 against an average of 91.28. Barley, 90.17 against an average of 90.3. Oats, 86 against an average of 86.8. Beans, 94 against an average of 91.34. Potatoes, 88.7 against an average of 91.64. Other roots than mangels, 82.2 against 87.8. The figures for this locality are : Cheshire.—Wheat 95, Barley 100, Oats 94, Beans —, Potatoes 91, Roots 91.

Lancashire.—Wheai 96,--B-a-I:ley 95, Oats 99, Beans 100, Potatoes 92, Roots 94.

Potato Prospects. Viewed from the point of percentage estimates the potato 'prospects are fairly good—about the same as last year, but three points below the average of the last ten years. There is, how- ever, another fact which is less reassuring— namely, that the average for July 1 works out to a lower figure, with the exception of last year, than any one year since 1903. On no occa- sion has the potato average worked out at less than 90 per cent. since the three poor potato years 1901-3, and now for two Julys in succes- sion we are in the eighties.

Figures and " Remarks" at Variance. The figures are more reassuring than the re- marks made on the margins of the reports. In many counties it appears that everything, was suffering from an intense drought; that swedes and turnips could not be sown; that many pieces of barley (after a second seeding) seem destined to produce nothing; that it is (or was) too dry for potatoes; that late-sown oats are very poor, and grass all burnt up. And yet we record fair average prospects--truly may we quote the old saying that "drought never brought dearth to England." We are to have a good wheat crop, but a poor hay crop, and as to roots, fodder, and grass, they need not be a cause of anxiety as they are all capable of making up for lost time.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LIFE OF SEEDS.

M. Becquerel, of Paris University, has sub- jected dried and stripped seeds of mustard, lucerne, wheat, and spores of asparagus, and various bacteria in vacuum tubes to the tem- perature of liquid air(-190 C.) for three weeks, and subsequently for seventy-seven hours to the temperature of liquid hydrogen (-250 C.). The seeds after one year and the spores after two years in a vacuum sprouted vigorously when ; sown. M. Becquerel concludes experimentally it is possible to suspend life for a period to which no limit can be set. Discussing Hie effect of the ' experiments, M, Becquerel points out that they first of all call in question the great principle of continuity, according to which life is nothing but a series of uninterrupted phenomena to which the slightest check is death. He points out that in future, by, adopting his method, it will perhaps be possible to keep the germs of certain forms of vegetable life in a state of sus- pended animation for many centuries.

CLOVER HAY. It is useless to allow grass and clover to stand after fully in flower, although during the time of inflorescence the plant makes its strongest efforts to grow. Trefoil soon passes the critical stage, and is the first of the hay crops. It is better cleared soon, and the land broken for roots, as trefoil makes but little aftermath. The remaining clover crops should also be mown and carried after they have bloomed, in order to make way for a second growth, which may be more successful. The clover crop is one of the most profitable of the rotation when it yields . one and a half to two tons per acre of good hay. It is obtained at little cost for seed and Sowing, and the return may be substantial. It derives benefit from previous close folding of roots, and isan excellent preparation for wheat. If its costs are confined to its actual cultivation it generally shows a better paper balance than either roots or corn; but in order to realise its advantages it must attain a satisfactory weight per acre. It is, however, extremely difficult to apportion costs to each crop of a rotation, and them in a rough and ready manner.—" Telegraph."

===== 0002601_19140718_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Keep the soil open with the hoe, and when possible give a mulch of manure around tall- growing plants, roses, etc. Train out the shoots of climbing plants, be- fore they become entangled. Prompt tying, thinning, and hoeing of Sweet Peas are the leading operations, and are likely to lead to better results than watering. Sprink- ling the planks in the early morning ,and even- ing will freshen the plants. When plants, such as Pyrethrums, Ponies, Oriental Poppies, and others have finished flow- ering, remove some of the old bottom foliage to reduce their bulk. This will let in light and air for the surrounding plants. As Lupins and Doronicums go out of flower cut off the flowering stems. The Lupins will, in all probability,- flower again in the autumn. 'Brompton Stocks are old-fashioned plants •that produce sweet-scented flowers. In years gone by they were highly valued, but nowa- days they are not much met with, except among the older generations of gardeners. The pre- sent is an excellent time for sowing seeds for plants to flower next year. A beautiful shrub, which is now covered with attractive white blossoms, of an orange blossom fragrance, is Syringa or Mock Orange. It is not difficult to cultivate. The suckers which are thrown up around the roots in October may be separated and planted. Roses have been going through a rough time, and now, after being subjected to cutting east winds the myriads of greenflies are playing havoc with them. Where these persistent pests have been allowed to work their pleasure scores of buds have fallen, all the vitality having been taken out of the flowering stalks. An every-day syringing with soapy water or ad- vertised insecticides, is necessary 'to reduce their number.

Running plants such as Verbena, Ivy-leaved Geraniums, Phlox Drurnmondii, should be pegged down to cover the surrounding soil. The Campanulas, or Bell Flowers, are good garden subjects, and soon provide material for division.

Sow Pyrethrum seed in boxes of fairly light soil and place in shade. Thin out the seedlings before they becomi, crowded and plant out in the autumn. If they can be wintered in a cold frame they will be, more likely to escape the slugs.

CAN'T BEAR TO HEAR YOUR CHILD CRY. Keep his stomach in order and you will soon find his temper improved. Next time he seems irritable, try a Steedrnan's Powder. Nothing else is just as good. Observe the, as in Steedrnan.

THE MOULTERS.

To encourage the moult, as I mentioned last month, the food should be reduced both in quantity and quality. But as soon as the first feathers are seen to drop the rations should be increased, for the formation of new feathers is obviously a strain upon the system. During the moulting period, which takes from six weeks to three months according to breed, age, and con- dition, the birds cannot be Said to be ill, but they are, nevertheless, passing through a some- what critical stage, when neglect may have serious consequences. If the weather is i.arm and dry, the process is hastened and the risk of illness reduced to a minimum. If, on the other hand, the days are wet and the nights cold, the new feathers take much longer to make their appearance, and unless the birds are kept away from rain they are very liable to contract colds, which may develop into more serious com- plaints. A generous proportion of green food is very beneficial, and should be supplied iiberally. Among the most suitable foods are meat meal, oats, and pea meal.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANCONA.

The Ancona, while it is bred fairly extensively in many parts of the country, is not a very popu- lar variety. In many respects, says a "Farm and Home" writer, it resembles very closely the Minorca, and the Leghorn. As a matter of fact, it is generally believed that the Ancona either is descended from the Leghorn, or else that these two varieties had a common ancestor. It is about twenty-five years ago since the first An- cones were imported into this country, but it was not until 1898 that a special breed club was formed. Unfortunately, the club has atssed into the entire control of fanciers, who regard economic qualities as of small importance, and who, to some extent, have damaged the value of the breed from a utility standpoint. It is as a layer that the Ancona is of most value to the farmer, for its edible qualities are poor. The eggs are white in colour and large, but they are of somewhat poor quality. The hens produce the bulk of their eggs during the spring and summer. The chickens are hardy, and exceed- ing precocious, developing very rapidly. In all respects, save one, the general appear- ance of the Ancona is very similar to that of the Leghorn; the point in which it differs is that it is not so, deep nor so prominent in the breast, giving it a somewhat slighter appearance. When the Ancona was first introduced the legs were yellow, mottled with black. but there has been a tendency to eliminate the black. The beak is long and fine, yellow in colour The head is small, the comb 'large, and the wattles fairly long. The back is round, the wings of medium size, and the body somewhat wedge-shaped. The plumage of both sexes, excepting the cock's tail, should be brown-black, each feather being tipped with creamy-white. The cock's c;ckles are white with black tips. ANNUAL SEDUMS.

Among the Many beautiful Stoneerops there are one or two annual kinds that should be included. Sedum annuum and S. cruleum are two of the best for the rock garden, both being perfectly hardy. They are often overlooked— why, it is difficult to understand, unless it is because they are not very well known. They give little trouble apart from sowing the seed, and although on dry soils they will often re- produce themselves annually, they never be- come weeds. At the present time they are a mass of flowers, from seedlings that have stood the winter in the open. Seed sown in spring AS ill (.011)P into bloom later.—" The' Garden."

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

Stone Sett Pavement for all Main

Roads.

CHILDREN AND ROAD DANGERS.

It is about time that serious steps were taken in this country to teach children the dangers of the road. In Berlin the school curriculum in- cludes instruction as to their actions in the streets, with a view to minimising the danger of children being run over. Among other things the children are instructed never to play in the roads where tramcars or other vehicles are fre- quently passing. They must also look out for approaching traffic in crossing the roads; never to go behind a tramcar to cross the road upon alighting, or never to run after or hang on to any carriage or cart. Such instruction has fre- quently been urged in this country, but the authorities seem slow to move in the matter, although the number of accidents continually occurring in the streets with tramcars alone emphasises the necessity for such instruction. This is the day of motor traction; hence our children should be educated in accordance.

Cleaning the All-black Cycle. Dangerous advice is often given with regard to the cleaning of the entirely enamelled or all- weather bicycle. It is suggested that this ma- chine can be cleaned by throwing water over it with a bucket or hosepipe, but the rider who follows this advice will probably find himself in grave difficulties with his bearings before long. However carefully made a bearing may be, water indiscriminately thrown about the ma- chine is sure to work in sooner or later. The best way to clean these machines is with a damp, but not too wet, stable sponge, finishing off by drying them with a soft linen duster. A machine that is ridden in all weather should be well lubricated, and special attention should be paid to the quality of the lubricating oil, which should be rather thick. The man who uses the same oil for his lamp and his bearings vrill find that the lamp gives a had light or that the bear- ings require much more frequent lubrication than they should.

The Use of Springs.

• During the past year or two motor manufac- turers have .devoted a considerable amount,of attention to the provision of shock absorbers, but as far as I am aware up to the present the cycle has received no such attention. Vibration is none the less present in cycles proportion- ately as in cars, and there can be no doubt that this is a recognised cause of fatigue Before the advent of the pneumatic tyre quite a num- ber of shock absorbing devices were placed on the market, and very good some of them were too, but the virtues of compressed air became hopelessly exaggerated and spring forks soon vanished. I should be the last to deny the ex- traordinary efficiency of the pneumatic tyre, but road vibration is still excessive despite im- proved saddle springs and better methods of manufacture. Formerly the great objection to spring forks and similar devices was the loss of lateral rigidity which always resulted, but as the principle is now universally applied to motor-cycles its extension to the ordinary bicycle presents no difficulty. In the meantime a very good plan is to have the front tyre rather softer than the back one. It has Tess weight to sup- port, and if this is done it hinders the vibration being transmitted to the handlebars.

A Cycle Tour. This month and for a couple of months to come one's thoughts turn naturally to the question of a summer holiday spent awheel. To one who has been accustomed to spending his vacation in this way for many years the only Considera- tion is with regard to actual details of the tour, such as prevailing winds, gradients, accommoda- tion, etc. But one of the principal charms of cycling is its suitability for people of both sexes and all ages; veterans can enjoy its advantages although for such a healthy pastime the num- ber of strained hearts and other physical dis- abilities resulting from its abuse may seem un- duly large. Every summer thousands of young people from counter and desk suddenly plunge into an exacting tour without the slightest pre- liminary training, and then Wonder why at the conclusion of the holiday finds them jaded and out of sorts. The distance to be covered is to my mind a matter of very little importance, and I would advise riders to be guided as to the length of the daily • run according to the con- dition of the roads, the weather, and last, but more important still, the physique of the rider. This is a point that should always be taken into account, since, if one overdoes it, the penalty to pay may be a severe one.

The Advantage of Plain Tyres. Our manufacturers and our motor-clir owners have been concerned during recent years with reference to the use of non-skid tyres that they taayip.repeess at urhcaltdovee(fi fail i el iitt aodiona not apo dtr evmcai neantt g tteolsi eiomfvapecrl ty those that te pptll that treads are the best for all purposes. Non-skid tyres were introduced principally for wet weather, and there can be but little doubt that the plain tyre is very much better during a spell of dry weather such as we have experienced recently. A tyre, the tread -of 'which is com- posed of rubber, is the only one that will grip the road when the surface is dry. Moreover, the wear is not so great on such a covering as with one that is composed of steel and rubber combined. It is a simple proposition to change the tyres on the back wheel when the car is fitted with detachable wheels, and only a few minutes are necessary for this operation. Even in the case of other cars it may take half an hour or over to make the exchange, but it is well worth the time spent, and this from eve - point of view. There is no reason why the steel- studded tyre should not be carried on the spare wheel

Paving Main Roads.

From time to time it has been suggested that all main roads should be paved with asphalt, wood blocks, or stone setts, and this especially on those thoroughfares where the traffic is very heavy. There can be no doubt, but that such roads would wear very much better than where tar-bound surfaces or water-bound macadam are used. I was interested in reading the other day an editorial by Lord Montague, in which he urges that tile main roads should be treated in one of the above ways. The cost would be con- siderable, amounting as it would to a sum of about £30,000,000, which at four per cent in- terest would represent £1,200,000 a year. A levy would have to be made on all road users, but the ratepayers would be recouped in that the cost of maintenance would be less. The crux of the whole question is given in the editorial as follows :

" Had we a statesman with courage enough to propose such a scheme, and a House of Commons wise enough to pass it, in a few years' time the roads of this country would consist of per- manent pavement, while the cost of operation of motor vehicles and the expense of maintenance would also similarly decrease at the same time."

A Manchester Reputation. The Royal Ruby Cycle is built with the idea that it has to maintain the reputation of Man- chester for high grade workmanship. Nothing cheap or shoddy is turned out at the Cannel- street factory. Amongst the models, one of the most taking is the Model "C" at .X 7 an instance of value which will take a deal of beat- ing. One of the Royal Ruby specialities is the disc-adjusting bottom bracket used in the high- est grade models, which does away entirely with cotter pins and enables the use of 5-16th steel balls instead of the ordinary 1-inch. The re- sult is very much easier running at a point where ease of propulsion is of the greatest im- portance. This bracket being dust-proof and Oil-retaining. perfect truth and sweetness of ru ant ng are assured.

MR. JOHN WARD'S SPEECH. The Exclusion of Ulster.

Mr. Joing WARD, M.P., was equally as cordi- ally received, and lie devoted himself to a review of the legislation passed by the Liberal Govern- ment since it came into power eight years ago. Their programme had been one for the demo- cracy, and had never before been equalled in history, and he ascribed a good deal of this democratic work to Mr. Lloyd George. Before Liberals, or even Labour men, could thoroughly grasp all that had been done they would have to study closely the last eight and a half years of Liberal legislation, and compare it with the , 20 years of "window dressing" by the Conser- vatives, when they were in power. While Mr. Lloyd George had raised more by taxation-250 millions this year—he had removed some of the taxes which the Tory party had talked about removing for 20 years, taxes which immediately affected the people. (Cheers.) The Parliament Act had made the way clear ' for Liberal legislation, but still, he thought, ' that required further amending before the Liberal party could get fair play in regard to their legislation. (Hear, hear.) The Second Chamber required amending and improving, and there was still a lot more work to be done in the cause of the people. There was one method of legislation when the Tories were in, and another when the Liberals were in power. It happened that if the Tories had only a majority of five members in the House of porn- mons they could get any Bill passed with the assistance of their friends in the House of Lords. If the Liberals had a majority of 100 they got their Bills rejected, and hence the Par- liament Act was to prevent that, yet that Act did not go far enough snd would have to be auended. (Hear, hear.)

HOME RULE MUST COME. The Home Rule Bill had passed nine times through the House of Commons, but still it was not law, as the Lords had again intervened. Despite the House of Lords, Home Rule would have to come. (Hear, hear.) Since it had last gone through the Commons, Ulster had made threats. They were told there were 100,000 armed men ready to fight if the Bill became law. They did not quite make clear who they were going to fight. All Liberals must condemn any such threat, and Ulster had no right to threaten to secure by force what they could not get as Constitutionalists and by constitutional means. The whole question was one that funda- mentally lent itself to constitutional methods, yet they saw the Ulstermen throwing over the Constitution, and a hundred thousand of them threatening to use force. What if that attitude and example set by the Ulstermen were to spread and the trade unionists, when they had a dispute, adopt it? There were four million trade unionists, with 16 millions of money, and if they were to adopt the same attitude as the Ulstermen they could force any Government out. Fortunately the trade unionists differed from the Irish in that they believed in constitutional methods, but had not these four million quite as much right to threaten as 100,000 Orangonien in Ulster? (Hear, hear.)

KNIGHT OF MERIT."

F.A.C.B. Ceremony In Warrington.

A large contingent of the Provincial Grand Lodge of Lancashire, Cheshire, and the Isle of Man of the Royal Antedeluvian Order of Buffa- loes assembled at the Tom Sangster Lodge (No. 1,418), Warrington, to take part in the ceremony of the exaltation of Bro. James Arthur Wood- head, C.P.C.G.H., to the rank of "Knight of Merit." Lodges from Widnes, Sutton, and Daresbury, and the combined lodges of War- rington were strongly represented, whilst the Knights of the Golden Horn (No. 59 Encamp- ment) were also present. Bro. J. Wolfenden, K.0.M., K.G.H., was the installing officer, and lie was-assisted. by Bro. J. Liston, R. 0.11., T. Davies, .R.0.H., K.G.H. (Directors of Cere- monies), Bro. W. Bradbury K.O.M.' K.G.H. (Provincial Grand Primo), Bro. J. W. Taylor, K. 0.31., K.G.H., and Bro. J. Walsh. K.0.M., K.G.H. (sponsors). Bro. W. H. Smalley, K.0.M., K.G.H., acted as Knight Bearer of-Jewel,, whilst Bro. E. Lawton, K.O.M, K.G.H., filled the role of bearer of the Insignia of Peace. The follow- ing were the sword bearers of the various de- grees. Third degree, Bro. J. Holden, K. 0.31., K.G.H.; J. Wilkinson, K.0.M., K.G.H.; W. IFl.ler, K.G.H.; J. E. Jackson, K.O.M„ Ezo. H. T. Hopkins. Second degree, Bros. W. Chadwick, C.P.; G. Bazlehurst, C.P.; J. T. Craig, C.P.; J. T. Harrison, C.P.' K.G.H.; G. Taylor, C.P:; .W. Haddock,. C.I. First degree, Bros J. Kennelwell, H. Adams, T. Hands, Cr. Clubley, D. McDougall. After the worthy Primo had extended a hearty welcome to the 'brethren the installation wag proceeded with, and was intently listened to by. the large assembly. Presentations to the newly-installed Knight were next taken, and these were made by Bro. J. Wolfenden, Bro. W. Bradbury, Bro. T. Day.les, and Bro. W. Rawson.

The ceremony over, convivialities followed. and those who contributed to the harmony of the evening were Bro. Gorse, C.P.; Bro. J. Hands; and Bro. G. Taylor, C.P. The toast to the new Knight was in the hands of Bro. J. Liston, R.0.H., who spoke in high terms of the regard they had for Bro..WOodhead, to which the new Knight briefly responded. Bro. J. E. Jackson, K.0.M., proposed the toast of. the " Installing Officer," and lie responded, thanking all present for their attention during the ceremony. The toast of " The Provincial Grand Lodge and the Knights Chapter" was sub- mitted by Bro. T. Davies, Bro. J. Wolfenden, K. 0.31., replying. "Our Visitors" wap given by Bro.. J. T. Craig, C.P., Bro. H. J. Jlopkins responding. Bro. R. Savory, C.P., was the W.P., whilst the toastmaster was Bro. W. Chadwick. C.P.

MILK AND DAIRIES BILL NOT TO BE SACRIFICED.

At a meeting of the Council of the Central and A ssociated Chambers of Agriculture, held in Westminster on Tuesday, Mr. C. Bathurst, 31.P.4 at the outset, said with regard to the Milk and Dairies Bill, that although it was not announced on Monday, the Government had decided to re- tain that Bill on their programme. It was not to be sacrificed.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 18, 1914.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
"ATTACKING A SYSTEM."

Progressive Labour League Speakers and the Land Question.

LANDOWNERS FAVOURED.

There was another large attendance at the weekly meeting of the Progressive Labour League at Bridge Foot on Monday, when Mr. M. Llewellyn presided and Mr. J. S. Devine, of Manchester, was the speaker.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
TO REMEDY EVILS.

- --- The CHAIRMAN referred .to the "12th" in II e- land, and said he was very glad to see that the Ulstermen had had the sense to be orderly. The more he read about .the whole situation in Ire- land the more convinced was he that the people in Belfast and Ulster were being gulled by men who ought to know better. The Tories made all sorts of statements of the "terrible things that were going to happen," but none of them came true. On various plat- forms they made their ridiculous statements, but lie was very glad to see that the working men of the country were studying the various questions for themselves and seeing which was right. The Liberal party were confident of the support of the people, because they had been getting to the bedrock of the evils which affected working men ever since they entered into power. And they were going on with their good work. They had now found that if they wante4l to get at the bottom of wealth they had to tackle the land question. Therefore, as working men they must listen to the arguments raised in regard to the question and reason matters out for themselves. As a result of the present land system the working classes were taxed up to the hilt. The commercial world was taxed far too heavily in order that the landowners could go free. The result was that working men and business men had to bear a burden which was far too heavy for them. The Liberal party were going to remedy many of the evils under which the people suffered, and when they came before ' the people at the next election they were hoping for the whole-hearted support of the .people. (Applause.) THE PROGRESSIVE PROGRAMME. ,

Mr. DEVINE dealt at length with the Irish' question and said they proposed to bring about, between England and Ireland, a union strong and true that should never cease until Great Britain ceased to be a nation. In regard to Welsh Disestablishment, what the Irishman had done for his Church the Welshman could do for his. Let them think that, as in the case of the Irish Church, they were now in the darkest hour before the dawn. (Hear, hear.) The Liberal party had a grand programme in front of them, and one of the first things they were going to remedy was the present land system. Some people said " Why do you interfere with land? Why don't you interfere with factories, shops, etc.?" He would say to those people "We have interfered with factories with very satisfantory results." The day had gone by when a man could say "This is mine. I can do as I like with it."

People Must Be Considered. The great masses of people had to be con- sidered before the few who held a monopoly in anything that was Nvait/ed by the people. When people were sent into the world they were given the right to live. The Lord did not give them factories or workshops, but the land, and the land only, and by that land they must live. It was limited in supply, and neither Conserva- tive, Socialist, or Liberal could say that in view of that fact it should not be used for the greatest good for the greatest number. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140718_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Landowners as Quick-change Artistes.

_ In the old days the landlords had wealth, power, and position, but they were no mere figure-heads. They had to provide the soldiers, were responsible for the keeping down of crime, and had to maintain the roads and bridges. They gave good value for what they got, but coming down through the ages they got rid of all their responsibilities like a quick-change ar- tiste on the stage. The agricultural labourers worked under far better conditions in the old days, but one morn- ing the people of England woke up and found the common land had all gone, and that indus- trialism had stepped in. After illustrating how the preser4 system afflicted all classes of workers in the country districts and in the towns, Mr. Devine outlined the Liberal party's programme for giving a minimum wage to the agricultural labourer, better houses for the people, security of tenure for the farmer and shopkeeper, a more just rating system, and added purchasing powers to local authorities. In conclusion, he said they were attacking a system and only a system. What was wrong in that system they would improve, and the people of England would be able to live brighter and happier lives than they had before. (Applause.) On the proposition of Mr. J. A. Heywood, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the speaker and chairman.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE CO-OP."

Opening of a New Branch in

Willis Street.

A NOVEL CEREMONY.

On Saturday, for the first time in the history of the " Co-op." movement in Warrington, the opening of a new branch of the society was attended with a ceremony. A procession had gone round the town prior to the formal open- ing, and when it arrived in Willis-street— where the new preinises are situated—a very large crowd had assembled. The ceremony :was performed by Mr. Jelin Upson, the president of the society, who was supported by Mr. W. E. Dudley, a C.W.S. director.

THE OPENING. Mr. Upson and. Conditions of Labour.

A very large audience assembled around the premises when Mr. JOHN UPSON mounted tile top of the char-a-bane, and, addressing the gathering from near the driver's seat, said he supposed the right word for such an occasion was " auspicious." (Hear, hear.) Not only was that new building an augury of good hope, but it was a source of congratulation to the architect, who was a Co-operative Wholesale Society architect, who had now erected hi i first building in Warrington. It was also a matter of congratulation to the builders—the C.W.S. building dePartment—as it was the first struc- ture they had erected for the Warrington Society. Further, it was a matter of con- gratulation to the committee who had :been responsible for the building, and to the inem- hers of the society, in so far as it was proof of , the continued prosperity and expansion of their work in the borough. lie was particularly delighted that that for- mal opening took place during his presidency, and he regardt,d it. as a very great mark of approval on the part of the committee that he should be asked to undertake that somewhat novel duty in the history of the Warrington Co- operative Society. Hitherto the branches had been opened informally, but be thought the day had gone by when they should do things sub-k rosa—(hear, hear)—and the time had come When' they could go opt in the open and show to the People of Warrington how deep and firm were the principles of co-operation upon the people. He believed the society's figures would con- vince the people of Warrington that the move- ment had not only come to stay, but to pro- gress, although until the principles they all held dear were firmly established in their midst there was room for expansion.

On an occasion like that they were preaching to the converted, so he asked them to hold a stronger and deeper faith in the spirit of co- operation. "What we want is perfection in our me`mbers,7 said Mr. Upson, and, he added, "we do not think that a man or woman is a true co-operator who takes things in an easy fashion and does not do his Or her utmost to carry out the principles of co-operation. All members of the society ought carefully to con- sider Whether the articlikey were purchasing had been produced un good conditions of labour—(hear, hear)—and made by men and women who were receiving a just reward for their labour. They ought not to find co-oper- ators purchasing from those who kept their shops open very late on Saturday night, and who imposed conditions Upon their employes that the co-operators would not tolerate for one moment in their own shops. A member of the Co-operative Society could say: If I buy an article made by the C.W.S. I am sure that the man who has made it has received a fair wage and has worked under good conditions as to hours of employment, and I have no tight—no moral right—to spend my money without know- ing how the article I buy has been produced, and under what conditions.'"

So when they were opening that splendid branch, when they were proving that their work was expanding, they asked not only that the co-operative movement should expand in the sphere of its 'operations, but that the co- operators themselves should deepen in their own breasts the spirit of co-operation, and so prove by their own actions, in spite of any little diffi- culty and grievance, that they were in essence and in truth co-operators in the highest sense of the word. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

CO-OPERATIVE WORK. Mr. W. E. DUDLEY asked if that branch was not a stalwart representative of co-operative possibilities. He thought they would agree that it was an ornament to that part of the town, and that co-operation was a distinct and useful ornament to the whole of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) He was tired of being told that they did not reach the poor; "for," he said, "we started with the poor and the poor have. lost their poverty. When the poor come into the co-operative movement they become more respectable and are provided in time with houses in which to live." Co-operation ad- vanced the interests of the people. They were not out to rob, but to enhance the benefits of

the people at large. He believed the society that had built that branch had set up an example to the builders of Warrington. The society not only sold an article over the counter, but pro- duced it. (Hear, hear.) They had as many as seventy factories and works, where ideal con- ditions were observed and where they produced ,t 8,000,000 value every year for the members. They had close upon £1,000,000 invested, and last year, in their banking department, they I had a turnover of about £170,000,000, an amount almost sufficient to cover the national debt.

- Mr. RICHARD VERNON, a representative of the C.W.S. Architects' Department, referred to the great difficulties that had to be overcome in building the foundations, but happily they had been successful.

After the speeches Mr. UPSON unlocked the door amid applause, and immediately there was a rush to enter the building.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION AND TEA.

Later the committee and guests proceeded to the offices of the Society in Cairo Street, where, in an upstairs room a substantial tea was pro- vided. At the conclusion of the repast there was a very pleasing ceremony, during which Mr. John Upson was presented with a hand- some gold watch. Mr. H. SINCLAIR, a member of the committee, presided, and amongst others present were Mr. Jarman, the secretary, and Mr. Atkinson, the manager. The Chairman said it was usual to present a gold key to the person who formally opened a new building, but in that case they were doing a better thing and presenting a gold watch. (Applause.) Mr. VERNON, on behalf of the C.W.S. Archi- tects' Department, in making the presentation, said that they thought a watch was more satisfactory than a key—certainly in time Mr. Upson would be able to look upon it as a friend. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. UPSON thanked them heartily for the kind words, and also for the gift. He felt hon- oured in having been entrusted with the open- ing of those new premises in Willis-street, and that was sufficient reward—he did not suppose any .of them were in the movement for what they got out of it. (Hear, hear.) He would treasure the watch all his life, as it would be something to look upon with pride when he thought of the occasion on which they were assembled that afternoon. At the last meeting of the committee he jocularly remarked that he wondered if they were going to provide a gold key for the President, but he never dreamt for a moment that the gold key would eventuate, and become, by some mysterious process, trans- ferred into a gold watch. (Laughter.) Short addresses were also given by Messrs. DCDLEY, WILLIAM WRIGHT, and T. Fratousox. The branch in Marsh-street is to be trans- formed into a butchering department, the new building fulfilling the previous purposes of the Marsh-street shop.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW PREMISES.

The new premises were opened almost a year to the day since the first measurements were taken. There is a very large window space in the front of the building, where the brick-work is composed of glazed green bricks, whilst inside the shop there is a green plaster covering on the walls. On the ground floor there is the shop and storeroom, whilst a storeroom is also pro- vided upstairs. In the shop the flooriiy, is of pitch-pine blocks, and the ceiling pitch-pine boards.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
RASH ON HANDS AND FEET.

Kiddicott, High-street, Crediton, Devon, Eng. —"My complaint came out in a red rash with little blisters of water, and it used to itch and burn fearfully. My bands were so bad I could not do anything for myself or the children. The eczema was all in between the fingers and joints. My bands and feet were hot, would itch more when I went to bed. Then my little girl came out all over in a red rash on her hands and face. It was like pimples with a watery bead which used to itch and burn fearfully. I had to tie her to keep her from scratching herself. I tried ointments, hut she got worse instead of better. Then my little boy got it. The rash developed into sores, as the children would scratch until it would bleed. They were home from school for nearly three months. - .

" Seeing the Cuticura advertisement, I sent for a sample of Cuticura Soap and Ointment. I found it very soothing and it stopped the itch- ing, so we were able to sleep at night. I bought more Cuticura Soap and Ointment and by the time I used two boxes of Cuticura Ointro.nit and three cakes of Cuticura Soap we were all well." (Signed) Mrs. Hammett, Jan. 26, 1914. Although Cuticura Soap and Ointment are sold by chemists throughout the world, a sample of each with 32-p. Skin Book will be sent post-free upon request. Address post-card: F. Newbery & Sons, 27, Charterhouse-square, London, E.C.

How to Grow Old.

On his ninety-third birthday the late Lord Strathcona was asked for the secret of old age. " The best way to live to an old age," he said, "is by not thinking about age at all, but by just going on doing your work."

GERMANS AND FEW RENTS.

The Homes Chaplain's Salary.

MINISTER'S STRONG VIEWS.

The chief matters discussed at the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of GuardiAns on Wednesday were those r.fie-Aing the religionl instruction at the institution and the l'adgate Cottage Homes. Mr. A. iolley presided ever a fair attendance of members.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
NONCONFORMIST MINISTER.

Rev. 11. Ogden Recommended.

At a meeting of the House Visiting Committee Mr. Dale and Mr. Garnett reported that as desired by the committee they bad, together with the Clerk, interviewed,the Rev. ff. Ogden on the question of the appointmert of a Non- conformist minister to the Workbag-se. The Rev. H. Ogden had been informed that the minimum duties connected ..ith such office would be as follows: The holding of an evening service weekly on a day other than Sunday ; cne weekly yisit at least in addition to the visit entailed by the holding of the- service and attendance on any sick calls forwarded to him at any time by the Master. The question of remuneration was discussed, and the sum of .226 per annum was suggested and provisionally agreed upon. The committee recommended that the Board be requested to appoint the Rev. H. Ogden as Nonconformist minister to the Workhouse upon the above terms and conditions.

Mr. DALE gave notice that he would move tho Rev. H. Ogden's appointment at the next meet- ing of the Guardians.

RELIGION IN THE HOUSE.

Large Attendances Justify Special Building.

At the same meeting of the house Committee the Chaplain (the Rev. W. Bracecamp) presented his half-yearly report, in which he stated that during the 12 years he had held the position the religious tone of the institution had never been better than at present... More people were at- tending the weekly service, and there were more requests from the inmates to talk to the Chap- lain on spiritual matters. The great need of the institution was. a separate room entirely set apart for religious service. To bring the Union up-to-date, so that it might compare favourably with other institutions of the same kind, a chapel was absolutely necessary for the inmates and nursing staff. The service in the dining- room, where concerts and other entertainments were held, could never really be a success. It was decided that the report be considered at the next meeting of the committee, the Clerk to obtain, in the meantime, particulars from Unions of a similar size in the North of Eng- land of their arrangements for divine service. The CHAIRMAN said that the matter of the new church owould come up again. The proposal received the approval of most of the members Present at the committee meeting.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
A UNION FIRE BRIGADE.

The Master of the Workhouse, in his half- yearly report, suggested that a fire brigade, com- posed of the male staff, be established, and that the Superintendent of the town Fire Brigade be asked to take charge. The suggestion was approved, .and the Master was authorised to have such of the apparatus as he found defective repaired and replaced. The report was adopted. \ NO PEW RENTS.

Rev. A. M. Mitchell and Free Services. At a meeting of the Cottage Homes Commit- tee, Mr. Mather proposed and Mr. Dennett seconded, the appointment of the Rev. H. F. S. Collier as chaplain at the homes at the rate of £lO per annum, together with the sum of £l5 per annum for pew rents. An amendment was moved by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell and seconded by Mr. Dale, to the effect that Mr. Collier be appointed at a salary of 1-25 per annum. The amendment was defeated by four votes to three, and the original proposition was approved. The Rev. A. M. llrraitu: We were fairly equally divided at the meeting yesterday. • I may say that we were unanimous as to the appointment, but I think the matter in regard to 'salary and pew rents demands careful con- sideration. I think that we ought to agree on the matter now without having it before us again. There were some members absent, or I think we should have carried the amendment. The Chaplain receives a salary of £lO per year, which, to my mind, is an insult. To offer any priest of the Church the sum of £lO per year for the work to be done there is—well, as I have said, an insult to him. I, for my own part, in such a position would have declined to accept it and preferred to do the work free. gratis and for nothing. We are told the sum of £l5, pew rents, goes to the Chaplain. lam not satisfied as to that. Mr. Mather is not here just new, but he is a great authority on this matter and knows full well in regard to these pew rents. It is a well-known fact that Padgate, to-day, is a free church. The pew rents were abolished, and a certain sum was agreed to be raised by the people in lieu of pew rents—a sum of £6O. I take it that if the people of Padgate desire to give £6O in lieu of pew rents, they do not do it--

Flogging a Dead Horse. Mr. GARNETT : You are flogging a dead horse. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL: What I say is that this 4:15 ought to be salaried direct. The .children or anyone who go there have as much right to enter that church and ask for a seat free as Mr. Mather or the most influential parishioner in Padgate. The CHAIRMAN: Are you going to move an amendment? Mr. GARNETT : He has not got that far. The CHAIRMAN: Order! Order!! Mr. Garnett. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL: I am prepared to move in amendment that this matter be referred back. The CHAIRMAN : That the salary be—? " The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL: I move that the salary be ..1.15 and not relating to pew rents. Mr. DALE: I second: I do not believe in pew rents being paid in places of worship, or espe- cially tolls being plaeed on children in places of worship. The tHAIRMAN : The main issue is not in dis.. pute. It is as to the particular, mode in which you pay it. Mr. MCKINLF.T : That is the only thing. a tec.hnical difference. Miss BROADBENT : Would it not be better-- . Clear ad Definite. The CHAIRMAN: It is rather a question of wage. There is no dispute about the amount involved at all. It is for members to decide on the mat- ter before us now. The amendment is clear and definite. It is disapproving of the payment in the form of ',pew Ants. Mr. McRIKLEr : I think it is unfair to put the matter before those who were not present yes- terday. The CHAIRMAN. It is perfectly in order. The amendment was carried, the voting being: For the amendment, 7; against, 2; did not vote, 1.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JULY 18, 1914.

THE LORDS AS "LEGISLATORS." The Lords are sweeping on to their final and utter destruction. Given an opportunity of redeeming their charac- ter as ready-born legislators, they have made a more sorry exhibition over the Home Rule Amending Bill than any they have ever made in their long, rollicking and irresponsible career. They were asked to make Home Rule as palatable as possible to their friends; they have simply rejected it, but gone a roundabout way to do so. They have, in fact, killed the Bill twice over, just to make sure that it is dead. First they provide for a "clean cut," for which Sir EDWARD CARSON asks with respect to Ulster, and the Unionists are to have Nationalist as well as Orange Ulster; they have dispensed with the suggested appeal to the people of Ulster on a question so vitally affecting themselves; they have gone beyond Ulster and re- moved the judiciary from the appoint- ment and control of the Irish Executive. That was what they did officially, andl it was sufficient for all their purposes.l But an unofficial resolution, introduced ostensibly in the name of friendship,' was passed, which arranged for the suspension of the Home Rule Act alto- gether, "until a Commission shall have reported on the constitutional relations of Ireland to the other parts of the United Kingdom." That takes us back with a swing to the days prior to 1885, since which date all parties have been considering the relations between the two countries, until they are very weary of the job. That is the way thei Lords deal with Liberal Bills. Reject them or hang them—anything but pasS them. In these days of the operationl of the Parliament Act they adopt the latter alternative and trust to luck for the rest. It was in vain that Lord CREWE pointed out that the Opposition had ex- eluded from the Home Rule area Roman Catholic counties in which the demand for Home Rule was not less earnest than the demand for exclusion in the North-east counties. The Oppo- sition leaders, he argued, seemed to' forget that, just as they could not undertake a solution which left out Sir 1 EDWARD CARSON and his followers, SO I the Government were equally bound to consider the views of their followers. Lord LANSDOWNE based his defence of the "clear cut" on the ground that nothing less would restrain the Ulster Volunteers from flying to arms. He argued that the Home Rule Bill had "had the. bottom knocked out of it" by the Opposition amendment to exclude the whole of Ulster. That, at all events, was a candid confession that the Lords had done what they set out to do—not to conciliate Ulster, but to knock the bottom out of Home Rule. That is the aim and object of the Ulster hubbub ; we have known it all the time, but it is Satisfactory to have our impressions confirmed by so competent an authority. The Commons will deal \vitt' the Lords and their proposals on Monday, and the public will do well to suspend judgment until then. We need not be too deeply impressed by the pictures of machine guns on jaunting cars drawn through the streets of Belfast to be Photographed for the English news- pakers. That is not war; not even civil war. Those are most successful in fighting who keep their plans to themselves and their strength unknown to the enemy longest. A General would sooner think of committing suicide than let 'At theis plaNns escape before he acted. Bailey h Worst it is a political Barnum and ow, indecently impressed upon noticeOUrs when efforts are being made to find a !peaceful solu- tion of a trying problem it is a dan- gerous expedient, we admit, but desperate men adopt desperate methods. We shall not be unduly alarmed at the sight of the gun or the jaunting car—but we shall keep our eve steadily upon the statesmen who are trying to find the suitable Way out. That way will not be the Lords' way. Home Rule is as certain as that to-mor- row's sun will rise, to quote the memor- able words of Mr. REDMOND. It rests With Sir EDWARD CARSON and his friends, recognising that Orange ascendancy, if it still prevails in Belfast and its suburbs, cannot rea,sonably be allowed to cast its shadow upon a whole nation, in which Orangeism represents only a small, if somewhat arrogant, part, to do his part in effect- ing the settlement that must be made.

PASSING WEEK.

A WEARY STRIKE-AND THE WAY TO

PEACE.

The Tanners' Strike still drags on its dreary way. Both sides are probably tired of it, but, as is generally the case, when capital is pitted against labour, capital has the most endurance. The position means, in many cases, want fol the men and their families; to the masters only money loss, with them it is a long time before it reaches the bread and butter point. A number of the men have secured temporary employ- ment in other industrieS, and this has eased the situation in individual cases. The union were unable to distribute any strike pay this week. There are signs of breaking away on the part of some of the workers, And there is little doubt if the yards were open on Monday many would return to work on the masters' terms.

The masters, we know, regret the privation' caused, but they contend that the workers have brought it upon themselves by their unreason- able attitude, and that if privation results, it is as foolish as the hunger strike of the Suffra- gettes—it is self-imposed. On the other hand, the workers believe they are fighting for fundamental principles of truth. unionism, especially in regard to the reinstate- ment of all strikers. This is no doubt the real remaining difficulty. A fortnight's notice has ao nn ei se ts cbaeennnoatgreeudndtoer; wtohrekinpgreswiethntthecinrocun snti ceasonably be oblected to, and certainly the men cannot enforce it. The doubt as to reinstatement has made the difficulty for the men. They are uncertain is !o what it means. This condition may never be put in force; it may only apply to two or three men whom the masters contend keep their yards in a constant state of ferment ; or it may be meant to apply to all those men who have taken any prominent part in the present strike. It is a great pity, we think, that in their ultimatum the masters did not give some indication as to what it meant. It is the uncertainty created by this condition that is preventing a settlement of this unhappy strike. If our summary of the situation is the correct one, it seems that a settlement ought to be within reach of the parties to the dispute. ATI explanation for the sake of clearness being du? from the masters, they might reasonably be expected to make it. On the other points be men might concede the conditions, the masters having foregone their objections to treating with the union. This seems to us to be a fair basis upon which the parties might meet, with- out loss of dignity to either, to discims the full . terms of peace.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
„. • JOHN WARD, M.P., AND FUNDAMENTALS. As was to be expected of a man of Mr. John Ward's calibre-, he spoke on fundamentals, when ' he visited Hill Cliffe last Saturday afternoon to take part in the demonstration of Knutsford Division Liberals, at the invitation of Councillor Henry Roberts. The Labour Member for Stoke is of the Cromwellian type, and, like his great pattern, he tolerates trivialities only to show up the essentials with which he deals in no uncertain way. He incidentally mentioned that he once worked as a navvy on the Ship Canal, near Warrington, at 41d. an hour, which quali- fied him, now he is M.P., to speak for the people. One of his fundamentals is that the people have a right to govern themselves, and he treated with scorn the arrogant claim of the Lords to meddle with the Budget. The ques- tion submitted to the electors in 1910 was whether the Lords should be given authority to exercise their veto at the expense of the people, and the people of Knutsford backed the Lords! The thing seemed so funny that Mr. Ward laughed at his own words, and said nasty things about the people of the Knutsford Divi- sion. A small majority of the electors of War- rington also supported these ready-made legislators. It would be interesting to know what the founder of the Navvies' Union would say about Warrington if we invited him to speak here.

Mr. John Ward has also been a soldier, and he misses the fundamentals to success in the Orange "demonstrations.' He sees not war in them, but politics. He also sees something else, and asks what may happen if the example of lawlessness set by the great ones of the land is imitated by the trade unionists, who number 4,000,000. As a Socialist, he gave a significant warning. An armed struggle between the mil- lions with little and the few with millions would mean, when it was all over, a new start in the matter of possessions. The inference was that the "great ones" ought, in their own interests, to support law and order, not bring it into con- tempt.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL SOCIETY AND THE ORGAN

FUND.

The Musical Society are making a special effort to add to the fund that is being raised to buy an organ for Parr Hall. As Mr. Reynolds says in his appeal, which appears on our front page, the organ is urgently needed. It is to be hoped the Musical Society net will manage to bring in all the potential subscribers that the general net has missed, and assure the success of the movement. The society's list already shows a total of £4l 15s. 6d.

"KINEEMAS."

The Education Committee will be asked on Monday evening to join the Town Council in asking the Watch Committee to reconsider their decision not to forbid the presence of children under fourteen at cinema shows during the hours the elementary schools are open.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
TRINITY SCHOOL.

The managers of Trinity School have fallen foul-of the Education Committee and have inti- mated to the School Management Committee that they have taken certain action in regard to the infant& department. The School Manage- ment Committee have sent the managers a very pointed reply, taking Up a directly opposite position from that adopted by the managers. We refrain from publishing the minutes, n'sit would be unfair to give them as an exparte I statement; but we should say the discussion on them on Monday will provide our readers 'with some highly interesting reading for next week.

Dietetic Diagnosis.

_ _ Doctor's Wife: I notice, dear, you always particularly inquire what your patients eat. Does that help your diagnosis?

The Doctor : Well, my dear, in a way, yes. By the dinners they can afford I know how much I can risk sticking on the bill.

A Message from the Sea. Romantic Tripper: "Tell me, have you ever picked up any bottles on the beach?" Boatman: "Werry often, Miss!" Romantic Tripper: "And have you found anything in them?' Boatman: "Not a blessed drop, Miss!" —"Punch."

The Cat's Allowance.

Only one cat in the kingdom receives an allow- ance from the Treasury (says a correspondent), and this is the cat at the Record Office. The present cat, who receives sevenpence a week to be spent in cat's meat, is a pale brindle. He must have a good reputation as a ratter. He makes a tour of the Record Office at night with the staff.

The Initiated Debutante.

" That remark," said Senator Depew, as he dissected an opponent's argument at a dinner in Washington, " that remark was malapropos. "It was, in fact, so malapropos that it re. minded me of the dainty debutante, fresh as an April lawn, who cried out at her first dinner party :

- " ;This pudding tastes just like mustache cosmetic.'

" And then, when everybody stared at her, the debutante blushed as red as a peony."

UNREHEARSED.

Some in an oaken coffin sleep, Some rest beneath the wave, And some have lived, who vainly weep For those they could not save; While they, their calm composure keep For ever, in the grave. They searched the waters at early dawn,

-- - Where the tragedy began, And floating near on the tide was borne Our lost tragedian— Who clasped a relic, death had torn In parting wife and man. a Some little pride, as we read the page, Was mingled with our woe— For the actor's part, on a sinking stage. In a hurried farewell show— Where death doled out his pitiful wage, And every heart sank low.

Stern fate has rung the curtain down, And left but little trace Of the hero look, or the tragic frown, On the actor's quiet face; But a peaceful calm has laid thereon An unrehearsed grace.

That silent man, who last called out To bless a loyal friend— Those words of hope, 'mid fear and doubt, That on a wife did tend— Have played a manly programme out Unto the bitter end. J. NORBURY.

ALDERMAN TINNION'S WEDDING

- Quiet Service at Holy Trinity.

An interesting but quiet wedding took place at Holy Trinity Church early on Thursday morning, when Miss L. Saunders, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Saunders, of 8, Samuel-street, became the wife of Alderman D. Tinnion, of 18, Winmarleigh-street. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton officiated. The bride, who was given away by Mr. C. H. Page, wore a blue silk dress, with hat to match. The bridesmaid was Miss Pthel Saunders, niece of the bride, and she wore a navy blue costume and white hat trimmed with blue, and a gold bracelet, the gift of the bridegroom. Mr. Reginald Tinnion, son of the bridegroom, acted as best man. After the reception at a friend's house in Suez-street. Alderman and Mrs. Tinnion left for North Wales, where the honeymoon is being spent.

Many valuable presents have been received.

LIBERAL CLUB EXTENSION

The John Crosfield Memorial Hall.

OPENED NEXT THURSDAY.

Next Thursday will be a red-letter day in the history of local Liberalism, for Mrs. John Crosfield will open the mag- nificent Memorial Hall she has had erected as an extension of the Liberal Club, in memory of her late husband, Mr. John Crosfleld. The proceedings will begin at three o'clock, and the following programme will be carried out :

The Chairman's Opening Address— Mr. C. E. Parker. Presentation of Key to Mrs. John Crosfield by Mr. A. Warburton, the Architect of the Hall. Mrs. John Crosfield will declare the premises duly opened. Presentation of bouquet to Mrs. Cros- field by Mrs. Ziba Armitage. Adjournment to the Memorial Hall. Vote of thanks to Mrs. Crosfield, proposed by Alderman Tinnion, seconded by Councillor John Wilkinson. Presentation to Mrs. Crosfield by Mrs. Osborne, on behalf of the Women's Liberal Association. Mrs. Crosfleld's reply. Addresses by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, President of the Club ; Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, Presi- dent of the Women's Liberal Association ; Mr. C. E. Parker, President of the Liberal Association ; Mr. E.. Wynne Jones, Presi- dent of the Progressive Labour League. Inspection of the rest of the building, and then of the Club premises and the bowling green. Selections of music by Crosfield's Prize Band. Afternoon tea at 4.15. Building open for general inspection, 6.30 to 8.30.
