HALL NOOK ESTATE.

Forthcoming Important Property Bales.

One of the largest property sales that have taken place in this district for a considerable period is advertised in to-day's " Examiner." It will take place at the Blue Bell Inn, Horsemar- ket-street, Warrington, on Wednesday, July 29th, at 4 p.m., when the Hall Nook estate, and valuable freehold farms, building land, ground rents, and freehold and leasehold dwelling- houses, shops, and warehouses at Penketh, Great Sankey, and Warrington. .will be placed under the hammer by Messrs. -A, H. Clive and Co., in conjunction with Mr. Robert Lowe. The first lot is the attractive freehold country residence, known as "Hall Nook," Penketh, formerly the residence of the late owner, Mr. Robert Garnett, with extensive gardens and grounds. The total area is about four statute acres. The internal arrangements are of the most complete character, the house having been finished and fitted by the late Mr. Garnett in the best manner, and with great taste. As a low reserve has been fixed to ensure a sale, an, un- usual opportunity is afforded of securing a modern residence within easy reach of important business centres at a very moderate price. The other lots consist of farms, residences, fields, cottages and gardens, and ground rents, particulars of which are set out in the advertise- ment and more fully in the particulars of sale. A point is made of the fact that the properties are for occupation or investment, that there are low reserves to ensure a sale, and that the vendors are prepared to lend a proportion of the purchase money on mortgage. Further particulars and plans (of Lots 1 to 15) may be had on application to Mitchell and Bunting, chartered accountants, 22; Lord-street, Liverpool; Robert Garnett, surveyor, Bold- street; A. 11. Clive and Co., auctioneers, Egypt- street; Robert Lowe, auctioneer, Buttermarket- street ; Titchett and Son, estate agents, Cairo- street; or to Robert Davies and Co., solicitors, Warrington.

OPEN-AIR SERVICE.

The second in the series of monthly open-air services being held during' the summer by the Warrington Free Church Council took place on Sunday evening on the ground adjoining St. John's Church, Wilderspool-causeway. Jr.ll Thomas Dale presided over a large attendance, and inspiring evangelistic addresses were deliv- ered by the Revs. W. A. Beckett and William Reid. There were also present the Revs. T. T. James, H. Ogden, A. J. Chessum, and W. T. Jaye.

WARRINGTON P.S.A.

Mr. Roberts presided over a welhattemjed meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. in the Park Picture Palace on Sunday and gave a few re- marks upon " Christian Optimism." The ad- dress was delivered by Mr. William Evans, and dealt with "Great disappointments in Life." The lesson was read by Mr. W. Swaine, who also rendered a fine violin solo. Two solos by Miss. Secoodaru School's Successful Year

A PROFESSOR ON THE KITCHEN POINT OF VIEW IN EDUCATION.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
The Completed "Ladder" to the University.

Less wastage of brains That is to be the motto in Warrington for the future. The top- most rung of the educational ladder has been placed within reach of the poorest boy or girl who may now go from the elementary school right on to the university. This splendid scheme of university scholarships has been adopted for Warrington, and a great deal was said about it at the annual prize distribution in connection with the Municipal Secondary School, held in the Gymnasium on Thursday afternoon. There were many evidences that the school is filling a very important place in the educational and general life of the town.

Councillor H. Roberts (chairman of the Educa- tion Committee) presided, and the prizes were distributed by Professor Conway, of the Man- chester University, who also delivered a forceful and striking address to the students. Amongst those present on the platform were: The Mayor and Mayoress (Councillor and Mrs. Peter Pea- cock), Mr. T. Moore Murray (Director of Educa- tion), Mr E. S. A. Robson (headmaster), Miss Clark (senior mistress), Councillor Joseph, and Mr. E. Plinston.

ing to secure a more extended school life for their children, and last, but not least, to my colleagues on the teaching staff, who have worked loyally and harmoniously in the dis- charge of their duties. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140718_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
FACILITIES OFFERED BY THE

SCHOOL.

Great Things Expected from the Scholarship Scheme. The Chairman said that first of all he wished to express, on behalf of the Education Com- mittee, their high appreciation a the able manner in which the head and the whole of the teaching staff of the school had devoted them- selves to their duties during the past year. (Hear, hear.) He desired also, in a general way, to congratulate .very warmly indeed all the successful students, and last, but not least, he desired on behalf of the Education Committee to encourage those who, whilst not yet the holders of any particular distinction, would apply themselves diligently to the opportuni- ties which the teaching of the school afforded, in order that later on, whether they occupied positions of prominence or not, they would, with the cultivated minds and education they received there be able to apply themselves to those social and complex problems better than the past generation had done and with .which every generation always met. Referring to the scheme for providing a limited number of university scholarships, the Chairman said they claimed that there was an opportunity at least for a few, no matter bow humble their positions might be, to go up the ladder from the elementary school right into the university. They were expecting great things from the scheme. He would like to say that he did not know any scheme in regard to which an expenditure of money had been in- volved that had gone through in Warrington with so little adverse criticism and so much general approbation. He thought he might say that that spoke well for the attitude of most !If the people in the town to that very great ques- tion of education. They were expecting very great things from that scheme, and the reason why he mentioned it at that juncture was that they had had invaluable help and assistance from such an experienced man as Professor Conway, who was deserving of their best thanks. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19140718_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. ROBSON AND THE SCHOOL RECORD.

In presenting his tenth annual report the Headmaster (Mr. E. S. A. Robson) stated: The pupils attending the school during the session 1913-14 numbered 220 (133 girls and 87 boys), compared with 218 (133 gills and 85 boys) last year; ove; 130 candidates presented themselves for admission and 60 of these were accepted. The proportion of the sexes—three girls to every two boys—has been maintained for some time past and is due to the preponderance of girls in the upper forms—i.e., Form VI., Bur- sars and Student Teachers. Since the estab- lishment of the school in 1903 about 680 pupils have passed through, and it is a fact worthy of notice that whereas, at first, parents used to send their children for a period of one or two years in order to finish"" their education, it is now compulsory for all pupils to stay at least three years, and the average duration of school life for the past session is 3 years 11 months.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
Losses of the Year.

I have to record two changes in the teaching staff, which took place during the first term. The tragic death of the Physics Master, Mr. W. Donaghy, M.Sc., is no doubt fresh in the minds of all of us, and his splendid character and high ideals will remain as a lasting memory among his colleagues and old pupils. (Applause.) The resignation of the Chemistry Master, Mr. Paul Fisher, B.Sc., was due to his appointment as manager of a chemical works, and his valu- able services as a teacher here, extending over a period of ten years, have been such as to com- mand our best wishes for success in his new sphere of employment. (Applause.) The vacan- cies thus created have been filled by the appoint- ment of Mr. B. R. W. Jukes, B.Sc. (honours), of the University of Wales, and late student of the Imperial College of Science and Technology, South Kensington, and by Mr. L. G. Rose, B.Sc. (Loudon), of the University College of Notting- ham.

Teaching Profession. It is with regret that I have again to announce a decrease in numbers of the pupils preparing for the teaching profession. This decrease is not confkned to this school, since the numbers for the whole country have dropped from 10,340 in the year 1907-8 to 4,308 in 1912-13. The Bursar System is apparently not at fault and is likely to continue for some time, but the Board of Education appeared to have overlooked the chief cause of the decline, viz., the insuffi- cient remuneration, especially in the case of boys, who are now seeking appointments in the various departments of the Civil Service.

Valuable Innovations. Two point S of interest in the life of the Secon- dary School during the past session may here be noted. A visit to France during the Easter vacation was made by a party of girls under the guidance of the Senior Mistress, Miss Clark, and the French Mistress, Miss Forsyth. The facilities thus afforded to our pupils of gaining a first-hand knowledge of the language and social life of the French people proved most stimulating, and if the visit is repeated next year an increase in numbers may reasonably be expected. The second innovation has been the establishment of a University Scholarship scheme for the secondary educational institu- tions in Warrington. During past years it has been disappointing for teachers to train pupils up to the standard of a University Entrance Examination only to find that the topmost rungs of the educational ladder were missing and the talents of the pupils lacked fruition. Now, thanks to the energetic action of the Chairman and other gentlemen on the Educa- tion Committee—not omitting the valuable ad- vice of Professor Conway in drafting the regu- lations—no pupil of real merit need have reason to complain of any neglect to further his educa- tion to the highest point -of his ability. As regards the social and athletic side of school life the pupils have exhibited great enthusiasm, the recently-formed swimming section showing an increase in numbers.

Honours. The honours list for the past year is printed on the programme, and I offer my congratula- tions to Miss Gladys Joynson, B.Sc. (Liverpool), upon her distinction as the tenth graduate from tile school during.the last three years. We have now secured four degrees with honours, and three of our pupils have been judged by their professors to be worthy of carrying on original scientific research. Of other results the pupils entered for the Northern Conjoint University Matriculation and the Junior Oxford Locals secured several distinctions.

Finally, I wish to express my best thanks to the Education Committee and to the Director of Education for their keen interest in the pro- gress of the school, also to the parents, many of whom -nave made great sacrifices in endeavour-

PROFESSOR CONWAY AND THE

END OF EDUCATION.

Why Do We Go to School?

After distributing the prizes Professor Con- way spoke of his acquaintance and friendship with the Chairman and said that all who came

across Councillor Roberts liked to renew and extend their acquaintance with him. (Applause.) He would like to ask them a riddle: " Why do we come to school?" (Laughter). That . question was not so easily answered as one might . think, and perhaps some of them might not be so very sorry if he gave it up and confessed that he could not answer it. (Laughter.) There were a good many answers given by a good many men as to why they should go to school, or, in other words, "What was the end of education?" The answer he remembered most—lie would not say it was the truest—was given by a man of very great wealth some years ago, and he was afraid it represented what could be called the unredeemed British point of view of education. The man had just presented a considerable sum of money to a new building at a college, and when asked to speak and encourage other people to do the same he said in effect "You all know I have not had much education. I have done pretty well without it, but we cannot all do without it." That was his idea of education. (Laughter.) It was a thing to keep stupid people out of the workhouse, to prevent people being quite so much a nuisance as they might be. They might think that was a curious point of view of education. He thought so himself, but he 'also thought that there were a great many people who had not grown beyond it. He would suggest rather a different way of answer- ing the question, and that was that they went to school or college in order to learn to be at home in the world, in God's'world : men, things, and creatures. A good many people would an- swer the question easily andisay, " Oh, yes, you go to school in order to learn how to earn your living, in order to learn how to make an in- come." They could never be at home in a house unless they know their way to the kitchen, larder, coal cellar, or other parts of the house. They could not be comfortable in any home that had not got either of the places he had mentioned. The people who thought that educa- tion was concerned only in enabling one to earn a living were those who thought that in living in a home they need not go beyond the kitchen. The kitchen point of view in regard to educa- tion was not very, uncommon. Could they think they had altogether outgrown the kitchen point of view, the point of view which looked upon the world simply as a place in which they passed their physical existence and made a cer- tain amount of money?

Large Classes and the Early Leaving Age. They belonged to a community that insisted upon children being taught in classes of 60, 70, or SO together. Were they struck by the remark in the Master's report in regard to the decline in the number of people wishing to enter the elementary teaching profession? He was quite familiar with the difficulty, and found that it was not Merely a question of emolument, but that the prospective teachers thought that they could not hope to make much of their work if they were set to deal with a crowd of 60 children in one class. Thep could never hope to give them the teaching they would wish so long as they insisted upon such large classes; they had not got beyond the kitchen view. He might ask, "Have we got beyond the kitchen point of view when we insist on children going to work at the age of 12?" That was a question which he could safely put to that audience, because he had be- fore him children of over 12 years of age, and parents who were making sacrifices in order that their children should not be lacking in educa- tion and should not come under the half-time system. Of course, the kitchen point of view was true so far as it went, but if they tried to live in the kitchen of a house and never went out of it, and were asked what they thought of the house they would sfiy it was rather mono- tonous. He would suggest to them that the world in which we lived and in which we ought to be at home was the great house with a great many rooms and with a marvellously wonderful view out of every window; that we had eyes to see it and that we should never let the windows be blocked up with the smoke, of which Lanca- shire seemed to be fond.

Questions to Ask Oneself. A question to put to themselves was "Whether their school career had been a success or a fail- ure?" That was rather an interesting question, even for young ones. Another question, equally interesting, was "Has it left me eager to go on with books or left me so thinking that I do not need these books any more?" He wanted that school as well as others to turn out a lot of Oliver Twists—always asking for more. He wanted them to go out into the tremendously big world and be successful. A great Greek philosopher had said "If virtue could be put into human form she would be so beautiful that everyone would be madly in love with her." He would like to see a little of that mad kind of love for knowledge when scholars left the school or college (Applause.)

The Example of the Past. They could not look at the world as home unt:

WPI.B

Parr's Bank..

Parr's Bank, Ltd., declare an interim dividend at the rate of 20 per cent. per annum, less income tax.

Scarlet Fever Epidemic. There were 91 cases of scarlet fever in the Warrington Hospital last week. Thirty-one new cases had been reported.

Births and Deaths. The births in Warrington last week numbered 48, giving a rate of 34.7, compared with a rate of 21.7 in the same week last year. The deaths totalled 24, giving a rate of 16.6, compared with 8.4 last year.

Market Prices. Warrington, Wednesday. Wheat, ss. per 701 b. Oats, 2s. 1(kl. to 3s. per 451 b. Wheat straw, 265. per ton. Oat straw, 255. to 265. per ton. Clover hay, £3 per ton. Meadow hay, £2 10s. per ton. Wheat firm; only small supplies offered. Oats firmly held at late prices; fair supplies.

Typewriting Demonstration. Last night an interesting typewriting demon- stration was held in Cairo-street Schools, when exhibitions were given by Miss Millicent Wood- ward, champion typist of the United Kingdom, and Miss Dorothy Swinton, of Warrington, both of whom were trained by Mr. F. Wilson Wood, Bold-street. Mr. J. R. Widdup presided over a large assembly, and gave an interesting address upon the history of typewriting.

Latchford Laundry Annual Outing. Blackpool was the venue of this year's annual trip, whea about 70 of the staff made the icur- ney, and on arrival at the seaside town found that the Erna had fully catered for them. Nothing was wanting, and the trip was voted a huge sucoess. Gatherings of this kind go far towards promoting a good feeling between the employer and the employed. Undoubtedly the success of the Latchford Steam Laundry is in no small measure due to the excellent all-round spirit of ,comradeship and the ideal conditions which prevail in every department of the estab- lishmeat.

Beer and Wine Trade Trip. The members of the Warrington Beer, Wine and Spirit Association had their 17th annual picnic on Thursday, when, conveyed in two motor char-a-bancs and a taxi-cab, about 65 paid a visit to Shrewsbury, passing through the most picturesque villages in Cheshire and Shropshire. Starting from the Royal Oak Hotel elii.jy.,tbu proceeded through Weaverkam, Tar- porley, Whitchurch and Wem, arriving at Shrewsbury about noon. Alter an 'excellent dinner, the return was made, touching North Wales and passing through Middle Ellesmere and Overton. Arriving at Wrexham three hours later, the party spent an enjoyable hour and a half there, and then continued the journey to Chaster. where tea was partaken. The com- mittee which organised the outing consisted of Messrs. T. Sangster, Royal Oak; T. Caldwell, Glassmakere Arms; J. Payton, Blue Back; F. Bonnell, Cattle Market Inn; T. Hackett, Stam- ford Anus; W. J. Shaw, Greyhound; J. Savoury, Hop Pole; T. Boscow, Wilderspool Ring-o'-Bells; B. G. Little, Pickering Arms; and Peter Lawless, secretary, Borough Arms.

Priory School Drawing Successes. The Royal Drawing Society examination was held at the above school during June. The following were the successful students :—lllus- trating Syllabus, Division V.; Elizabeth Arm- strong, second class, pass. Ancient Order of Foresters. The 210th quarterly district meeting was held at the Roebuck Hotel, Bridge-street, on Satur- day. D.C.R. Bro. S. B. Healey was in the chair. The Courts were represented by the fol- lowing delegates; Court 138, P.D.C.R. W. Hip kiss, P.C.R. E. A. Jones; Court 240, P.C.R. J. Davies, C.R. L. Dillon; Court 430, P.C.R. J. Gregory, P.C.R. E. Clayton, P.D.C.R. Councillor J. Chrimes; Court 431, P.C.R. W. Lightfoot, P.C.R. J. Smith; Court 461, P.C.R. J. Gatley; Court 475, P.D.C.R. T. Hollins, P.C.R. E. Hol- lins; Court 9,335, P.D.C.R. A. Tilling, and P.D.C.R. G. hiayhury; His Worship the Mayor, P.D.C.R. Councillor P. Peacock, district trea- surer; P.D.C.R. R. Thornley, district secretary; sup Ported by the following three district trus- tees—P.D.C.R. J. Walsh, P.D.C.R. H. James, and P.D.C.R. L. Davies; and Sister A. Corradine, P.C.R. P. Wilding, sen., and P.C.R. A. Baines. For the position of D.C.R. for nest term Court 138 nominated Bro. S. B. Healey, and Court 430 nominated Bro. J, Chrimes. For tho position of S.D.C.R. Court 240 nominated H. Emmerson, Court 430 E. A. Clayton, Court 431 H. Perdue, and Court 475 E. Hollins. The meeting approved of the formation of a Friendly Societies' Coun- cil for the town, and requested its representa- tives to support the same.

Stockton Heath Walking Day. Rain threatened to spoil the Stockton Heath Walking Day yesterday, but happily it stopped at threatening, for as the procession began to move from St Thomas' Church the downpour ceased and fine weather obtained for the re- mainder of the day. A short service was held in the Parish Church shortly after noon, when the Vicar (the Rev. J. Collins) gave an address. Accompanying the procession were the clergy and church and Sunday school officials, who had arranged everything admirably. The route was down to Wilderspool and back, and then round the district by way of Walton. The music was supplied by the Warrington Bluecoat School Band. The vehicles in the procession were lent by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co., Mr. Bowdon. Mr. Endersby, Mr. Parton, Mr. Jones, and Mr. Naylor. Mr. Rose was the chief marshal.. About 900 children were enter- tained to tea, and for this purpose the church room and the schools were requisitioned, the boys attending the former and the girls and ladies' Bible classes the latter. During the afternoon and evening the scholars enjoyed themselves in the Park behind the brewery, where sports and games fere indulged in.

BOXING.

BANK HOLIDAY COMPETITION.

The special committee appointed for the 9st. 4Th. competition at the Warrington A.C. Sports met at the club headquarters on Tuesday even- ing, Mr. T. Roberts presiding. Officials were appointed, and it was resolved that in the event of the entries exceeding eight it would be neces- sary to reduce them before Bank Holiday. The conditions are three 3-minute rounds, with intervals of one minute. It was further agreed that, should a preliminary contest be necessary, that two additional prizes be given, therefore every winner of a bout on Bank Holiday would be a prize-winner. Intending competitors are requested to enter as early as possible, for late entries will not be entertained.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 18, 1914.

WALKING DAY TRAGEDY.

Magistrates After Long Hearing Dismiss the Case. ,

ACCUSED SET FREE.

Much interest centred around a case heard yesterday in the Warrington Police Court, when the Mayor presided over a full Bench of magis- trates. Other justices present v, ere: Alderman Bolton, Alderman Pierpoint, Alderman James Evans, Dr. Joseph, Mr. A. J. Jolley, Mr. S. Wright, Mr. G. Pilling, and Mr. W. Furness. Alfred Charles Harris, 21, Thelwall-lane, was charged with feloniously killing his wife, Lena Harris, on Walking Day. Mr. A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk), prosecuted for the police, and Mr. T. S. Steel appeared on behalf of the accused. It will be recalled that the Coroner's jury nearly a fortnight ago returned an open verdict.

Mr. Holloway said that the Coroner's jury had returned an open verdict, but the jury did not have the privilege of hearing all the evidence as the police then had not completed their in- quiries. Since the inquest the facts of the case had been laid before the Director of Public Prosecutions, and, in consequence of his instruc• tions, the police had now to proceed with the case.

A WOMAN'S STORY. Hannah Lawless, living at 77, Thelwall-lane, repeated the evidence she gave at the inquest. When they got home at nine o'clock they found accused lying on the sofa. He got up and asked where his wife had been, and what time she called it to come home. She said : "It is not late; you know where I have been. He then ran at his wife and tried to strike her. He pushed her on a chair, but she got up and.stood on the hearthrug. Accused attempted to hit iris wife, but struck her (witness) instead tsf his wife. Then prisoner struck his wife and knocked her between the chair and the sewing inao.iiine. She had no chance to intervene. The Mayor: How did Mrs. Harris fall r--Back- wards.

Witness added that she saw accused strike his wife. It seemed as if be struck her on the face. Deceased said " Oh!"

Cross-e_xamined by Mr. Steel, witness said she had nothing to drink at Mrs. Harris's house in the morning. Deceased asked her to go iLto the house at night, as she seemed afraid.

"IT WASN'T LATE." Mr. Steel: Was that the moment he got off the sofa?—Yes.

_ It was late to come home at nine after leaving at_two.7lt wasn't late.

Was deceased all right?—Yes; deceased was not under the influence of drink. You said at the inquest she was a bit merry, but not drunk. Do you say that to-day?—Yes; I say it to-day. The Clerk: She was merry, but not with drink?—No.

Mr. Steel : Had she difficulty in getting off the b-amn?—No.

She jumped up in a passion when pushed down?—She was annoyed and rather upset. She was angry?—Yes. Did she appear us if she was going"" for him?

_ Do you mean she was going to show her math- Ority

The Mayor: What did she say when pushed on the chair?—That she would not be pushed down again.

-Mr. Steel : Did she move her hands about?— I did not take notice. '

- YOU did not take any particular notice at any time? You wanted to get out?—Yes; I wanted to go home. I was too upset to take notice. You were so upset you could not say Lether he struck her or not?—No.

_ You said at the inquest you :mild not say whether he struck her or not?—No. The Mayor: Had you anything to eat when you were out?—Only two bananas. Dr. R. Smith, the police surgeon, said he made a post-mortem examination on July sth, in the presence of Dr. Manson and Dr. Robinson, and found that the woman died from well-spread inemorrhage on the brain. Cross-examined: Do you think the injuries had hen caused at the same time?—l am doubt- ful. There were so many. If they were caused at the same time the deceased must have been knocked about a good deal. One act of violence could not have caused them?—No. There must have been several F—Yes. If this woman had fallen about in the street in a-case of intoxication it could have caused the injuries at any time within about 24 hours of death ?—Yes. By the Bench: When were the bruises caused? —Within 24 hours of death. Dr. Joseph: Does it mean any time within 24 hours?—A qua,rter of an hour, or any time within 24 hours.

Mr. Steel: Did you form an opinion that the injuries might have been caused by the woman falling about?—lt is possible. Do you say probable ?—I do not go so far. Do you think it was probable that the injuries occurred in the house?—No; I do not think so. I do not think the injuries were caused by what Mrs. Lawless described. I do not think all the external injuries occurred through what took place in the house. Mrs. Lawless said this man got his wife by the throat. Were there injuries?—No. Mr. Runaway She might have been gripped by the throat and show no injuries?—lt might be so. Mr. Steel: You, at the inquest, heard Lawless say that while deceased was on the floor prisoner seized her by the throat and shook her?—yes; and I gave evidence there was no injury to the throat. Mr. llallav,ay It is possible that even if the woman had been seized by the throat there would not have been marks when you made the post-mortem?—No; if there was only gentle pressure.

Mr. Steel: She said he shook violently?—lt must have been very gently. Mr. liallaway : The woman might have got her injuries by falling about the street, or in a football match? You do not know if there was a football match in which this woman took part?—No; it is the cricket season now. There might have been a cricket match in which this woman took part?—No answer. The lurmorrliage might have been caused by her falling about the streets?—No; it was too recent.

Dr. Manson said he attended at the post- mortem examination with Dr. Smith and Dr. Robinson, and he agreed with Dr. Smith's evi- dence. The hemorrhage may have been caused t,y_che fall..

Dr. Joseph: Do you think it was caused by a violent fall?—There must have been very con- siderable violence. Mr. Hallaway : Could the injury have been caused by the woman tripping over the hearth- rug?—lt is possible.

WHAT THE DETECTIVE FOUND. Detective-Sergeant Holland said he investi- gated the case. He saw the body of the woman was marked and he inquired how the dead woman came by the marks. Hannah Lawless then made a statement, and as she was finish- ing prisoner came in. It was read over to Law- less in prisoner's presence: Harris said : "It's true." Later he said: "I have had some trouble since I married her, but I never thought it would come to this." Harris, at the police station, added: " I cannot say anything; it is too horrible." Since then he had been charged with man- slaughter, and he replied: "I did not do it." The Mayor: You did not caution the prisoner at the houseP—No, sir. Mr. Steel: You were at the inquest and heard the jury return a verdict that there was not sufficient evidence to show how she came by the injuries which caused her death?—Yes, that is so.

Did you hear the Foreman say the jury of 15 were unanimous?—Yes, I believe so.

Mr. Hallaway, re-examining, asked why he had not cautioned the accused at the house?—lt was not usual.

There was nothing, said witness, to lead him at first to suspect foul play. Had accused denied .the statement of Lawless he would not have taken him to the police station. He had no occasion to suspect him before reading Mrs. Lawless's statement. DISMISSED. The Bench retired, and at the end of a quarter of an hour returned to the Court.

The Mayor: We have CleClire'd that there is not a prima facie case tigainst Harris. (A mem- ber of the public in Court: Hear, hear.) ,

The Clerk: Then you discharge the accused? The 11fayor: Yes.

The Clerk: Harris, you are discharged.

Another Great Effort for the Infirmary.

MEDICAL RESEARCH AND COMMERCIAL ADVANCEMENT.

" Ye Old English Fair," in the Victoria Park, should bring in a wealth of coppers to the Infir- mary and other kindred charities. Two years ago 50,000 people visited the fair and on this occasion it is hoped that record will be exceeded. There are endless attractions to entice the coins for this good cause from the pockets of the public, including steam yachts, roundabouts, motor cars, &c. So far the weather has not been very Propitious, but it is hoped that to-day's fair will entirely make up for any deficiencies on Thursday and yesterday. The cause of the Infir- mary and kindred charities are very well known, and it is only hoped that the people will realise to the full what these institutions are doing, and flock to the fair this afternoon, when the opening ceremony will be performed by Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. and the chairman will be Colonel Charles Lyon.

be remembered by everyone. They were for- tunate in having one or two families in War- rington who were intimately linked up with it, and did so much for it, and Mr. and Mrs. Cros- field were only too delighted to do what they could for the Infirmary and every good cause in Warrington. (Applause.)

MR. A. 11. CROSFIELD OPENS

YESTERDAY.

The Value of Fitness.

A gloomy morning with continuous rain was far'from promising yesterday, but shortly before the time fixed for the opening ceremony, per- formed by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, the rain ceased, and shortly after four o'clock the sun strove hard to make the fair as pleasant as possible. In the absence of the Mayor, Mr. J. H. Smeth- urst presided, and he was supported by Mrs. John Crosfield (Colwyn Bay), Mrs. Osborne, Mrs. Ziba Armitage, Aldermen Smethurst and Bennett, Messrs. R. C. Parr, Henry Collin, J. Critchley, G. Bowman, 11. Rogers, C. A. Berry, W. Taylor, C. E. Houghton, G. Steeples, and J. H. J. Hampson,

===== 0002601_19140718_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
FRIENDS OF THE INFIRMARY.

The CHAIRMAN said the Mayor had telephoned from the Police Court, where he was detained, hearing an important case, and said how sorry he was he could not be present at the opening, hut hoped to be there some time during the afternoon. Mr. Crosfield represented a family which had done a very great deal for Warring- ton. (Hear, hear.) Their name would always

that the man in the street really did not quite realise what it meant from a commercial, and also from a national point of view to keep fit.

March of Medical Science. Take, for instance, the British Empire in India. It was not many years since they had soldiers in India invalided home in their thou- sands. They were invalided home still, but in their hundreds. That was a marked instance of what the advance in medical surgery and in every blessed art of healing had done for the people. People used to fear going out to India —the fear of typhoid kept people away in the old days, just as Yellow Jack kept them away from the West Indies. Now, however, typhoid in India and iellow Jack were things of the past, and the commercial man of means did not fear spending a short time in India any snore than he did a trip to Brighton. They got men of prominence taking their capital, and civilised developments to those different parts of the Empire, and he would say: All honour to the distinguished and eminent medical men, who, by their research and by grappling with the dis- ease and the microbe, have paved the way for advance in commerce and• civilisation." They had done the research work, but they must not forget the modern capitalist, who was public- spirited enough to find some of the sinews of war needed for that development. They should not forget the splendid manner in which medi- cal research had been carried out by the aid of Sir William Lever and Sir Alfred Jones, of Liverpool.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
Get Fit and Keep Fit.

_ Preaching on the text " to_ get fit," he would say that it was a duty that one citizen owed to another, a brother to his sister, and a husband to his wife, because it made their tempers better. (Laughter.) To keep fit was a fine prin- ciple for which to strive, and he hoped that in spending their pennies they would think they were helping those stricken down to enjoy the repose and obtain the assistance necessary for their complete restoration. (Applause.) There was no institution in Warrington that could appeal more to any of them than the Infirmary. He thought it was a matter for the deepest sat- isfaction that Church and Chapel, Tory and Radical, Protestant and Catholic, could join hands in supporting a great public cause, and he thought, that in itself was a good thing. TANNERS' STRIKE.

Deadlock Still Unrelieved.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
FRUITLESS MEETINGS.

The strike in the tanning world still proceeds aid both sides seem as determined as ever.

THE MEN REJECT THE MASTERS' TERMS.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Proposals Criticised.

The men held an important meeting in the Co-operative Hall on Tuesday evening and unani- mously decided to reject the terms offered by the masters, which were published in the "Ex aminer " of last week.

Mr. E. Marshall presided over a meeting nearly 1,000 strong. He said that he had hoped to have something definite to report, but nothing further had come from the master's side. All the same they had decided to call the men together to decide what they should do.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
ORGANISING SECRETARY'S VIEWS.

Mr. H. Harrison, the local organising secre- tary, said what had already been published gave a fair idea of the conditions the employers had laid down. They said they still were open to negotiate, but the men had unanimously passed a resolution declining the conditions of the masters. (Hear, hear.) They did not know what the employers would do in the future. The employers' manifesto stated that the dis- pute could not come to a conclusion unless a settlement of the trouble in Liverpool was also arrived at. There were two interpretations of that. .It might only apply to the general conditions laid down with regard to the giving cf notice to non-union men and the question of reinstatement. But there was another danger. ' The Liverpool men received wages far in ad- vance of those in the Warrington area. Per- haps it was the intention of the employers to have a basis with regard to that. Was the idea feasible that they meant to lift up the War- rington men to the standard that the Liverpool men enjoyed? Did they not think it more feas- ible that the employers were going to use the Warrington men—if they were allowed—as a means of dragging the Liverpool men down to their level? If they succeeded in that the War- rington men's aspirations to raise themselves would be gone.

The Notice Question.

With regard to the notice question he could tell them candidly that the Liverpool men would never consent to 14 days. (Applause.) Apart from whatever attitude the Warrington men might take up, the Liverpool men were not prepared to give more than seven days' notice. (Ap- plause.) In conference some time ago the Liver- pool masters were satisfied with seven days' notice, and it was only through the Warrington workers consenting, in a conciliatory spirit, to offer 14 days, that the idea had been put into their heads.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Non-Union Point.

On the non-union question the employers had shifted ground. The masters had told them that they could not make any distinction be- tween union and non-union men. It was a well- known fact that the men did not want them to make any distinction, but, unfortunately for them, the employers did make it. The two men at Astmoor tannery, through whom it Was claimed that the trouble originated, had privi- leges—in fact men were sent off their own work at dinner-time, while those two were allowed to work nearly all night. at the same job. They' could quote many instances.

Reinstatement.

On the question of reinstatement the em- ployers claimed that they could not take back all the men they had in their employ prior to the dispute. It either meant a dirty, deliberate attack upon the old men, who were, perhaps, not of the same marketable value as the young ones, or that the men who had taken the initia- tive in that struggle were not going to be taken back.

Personally, he inclined to the latter interpre- tation. If such victimisation were to be allowed it meant "good-bye" to the hopes of the Men getting anything in future.

Appeal to the Men. I appeal to you, my fellow workers, added Mr. Harrison, to stand loyally to the resolution you passed at your last meeting. Do you want to go back on it? (Loud cries of "No!') , The situation, he added, was entirely un- changed. The employers were evidently sitting on the fence, waiting for the men to climb down. His advice to the men was to wait on the fence and let the employers climb down. (Applause.) If they went back on the conditions the em- ployers proposed they would he returning to what would practically amount to slavery. They would not be allowed to speak. They already knew of rules operating in one shop where, if a man swore he was liable to dismissal. (Laughter.) I am afraid, added Mr. Harrison, that if a man used the language I use at times he would be hanged, drawn, and quartered. (Laughter.) We now know what we have to face and we must face it standing shoulder to shoulder. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140718_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
"SHOWN SELF-RESTRAINT."

Mr. C. ip-tikeii- said that if there ...s any weak- ening it was hot on the part of the men. (Ap- plause.) They had shown great determination and self-restraint. (A voice: "It may not last.") In Their negotiations the men had shown the greatest reasonableness, but had the employers? (Cries of "No.") If, however, the men con- ceded the conditions asked for by the masters there would be nothing to confer about. If they gave the employers all they asked, why meet them? They (the men) would be beaten when the employers sought to impose upon them con- ditions which amounted to an absolute sur- render; the only mandate that could emanate from the men was a blank refusal to consider the proposals until they were prepared to meet the men's representatives. (Applause.) The condition as to no distinction between union and non-union men smacked of imbecility. The fact was that there was no impartial view. The employers could no more treat union and non-union men alike on a basis of equity than the men could be expected to treat the non- unionist or " blackleg" with the same brotherly feeling that they would treat a comrade. The men would not tie themselves in their future i activities by guaranteeing not to take action against non-unionists. As to reinstatement, the safety of the men lay in refusing any form of settlement unless every man was guaranteed his job back again. They had non-union . eTaheanattltiaerehew no intention of molesting any man, but what amsoalecstleadu.se they meant to do was to see that soon there was not a non-union man in Warrington. They would then be able to show the tanning em- ployers and others how great was their power. (Loud applause.)

GENERAL SECRETARY'S VIEWS. Mr. E. Ormsby, the general secretary of the Tanners' Union, said that the Liverpool men were filled with admiration at the stand made by the Warrington men. Liverpool had taken an identical stand, realising that whatever vic- toofijuywmatiosacnl withiie v Warrington.edwoul Thedbeshare same dbythein capplied to the Runcorn district. With regard to reinstatement, the men real- ised that they could not all restart work on the same day, and they were prepared, to a certain extent, to accept such a paragraph as the em- ployers proposed on condition that no new em- ploye was engaged until the whole of the men who came out on strike were back at work in the tanneries. (Applause.) The men were asked to agree not to coerce anyone into joining the union, and further to undertake that they would not declare a strike if men refused to join of their own free will. Under such conditions they might as well put the shutters up at once. (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19140718_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Office Staff "Loyal."

"Where are the non-union men that the em- ployers speak about?" asked Mr. Ormsby. "There are no non-union men in the yards, ex- cept the office staff, and the masters are at liberty to call them ' loyal' if they like. It does not trouble us what they call them." He was certain that the men showed the same determination in their resistance to the mastet's latest as they bad shown formerly the masters would hove to realise that they would have to modify their terms before there was ally

chance of a settlement. It was a question of time. The men had demonstrated in no uncer- thin way that the "starvation trick" was played out, and they had taught the employers a lesson they had never had before. It would be a long time before the employers would again dally with any grievances or com- plaints that the men might have to present. MASTERS' TERMS REJECTED.

The following resolution was adopted-unani- mously:— "We are determined to stand together in resenting the employers' terms, and we reject the employers' proposals en bloc until they are prepared to meet the representatives of the men."

===== 0002601_19140718_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR LEADER ON STRIKES.

Mr. F. 11. Rose Says They Impoverish Everybody but the Masters.

Mr. F. H. Rose, in "A Workers' Notebook" in the "Manchester Evening News," says : Labour is again -upon the threshold of a familiar repetition of industrial history. There is little room for doubt that the trade boom of the past few years is,passing and the lean season of de- pression is at hand. Short time in the cotton trade, rendered necessary, as usual, by reckless over-production, is an accomplished fact; the vacant books are enrolling signatories; suspen- sions for slackness are common in many trades; all the premonitory phenomena of an approach- ing bad time are indicated in the signs of the times, and organised labour, as usual, is no better prepared to meet it. There has been 'a remarkable recrudescence ,if the strike evil since and including 1911. What is termed industrial unrest has manifested itself in hopelessly abortive efforts to force industrial betterment forward by the apparently contra- dictory method of suspending industry. In less than twenty per cent, of these strikes has there been even ostensible success for the workers; the remainder have been either shabby com- promises or downright routs. With regard to their cost to the workers, and to the nation, directly and indirectly, it must amount to more , than „t 50,000,000. Yet the increase of wealth, as shown by the income tax estimates, has ex- ceeded this sum considerably. What has been conclusively established is that the strike impoverishes, both immediately and permanently, all members of the community except the capitalist against whom it is directed. No advocate of the strike policy can say that the method has not had a fair trial. It is a curious reflection that in spite of all this failure the strike policy remains so popular. There is an idea that these continually aboi- tire attacks upon capitalism have made em- ployers afraid and that a quite new disposition on the part of employers has been caused as a consequence. This is the strangest symptom of this malignant strike disease. The disputes of the present day make folly of it.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE MACHINERY ; LESS LABOUR.

The "Leather Trades Review" says The end of the fifth week sees no improvement in the position here, and both sides seem deter- mined to fight the matter to the bitter end. There are symptoms of a more bellicose spirit in a section of the strikers since the publication of the masters' conditions, and if they are able to. impose their will upon the majority of the men the struggle may be prolonged. On the other hand, many of the strikers have the appearance of being heartily sick of things and tired of hanging about, added to which priva- tion is undoubtedly rife amongst them, and if it should be that the employers decided to open their gates at a given date it would be no sur- prise to find a good proportion of the men glad to get back on the old terms. The conditions which the employers demand shall be accepted before any conference are neither onerous nor humiliating, the first condition simply requir- ing that, in future, 14 days' notice shall be given before leaving work, and the second that, whilst the employers have no objection to their men joining the union, they require the union to give an assurance that there shall be no coer- cion of men into joining, and although this may be rather a bitter pill for the more ardent unionists to swallow there is nothing unfair about it. It seems, however, as though the great stumbling-block is in the third condition, which states that all the men cannot be rein- stated, as some of the works will not require so many men in future, and that the masters reserve the right to say whom they will or will not take back. This has given rise to an idea that the employers intend to victimise some of the more prominent figures in the strike and drive them from the town; but the real reason is that some of the yards have been putting in snore machinery, thus reducing. the amount of manual labour required, and also minimising the risk in the future of such a dislocation of business as exists at present.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
BUSINESS CURTAILED AT LIVER-

POOL.

The "Leather World," under the head "Liverpool (Hides, Salted Foreign)," says— Business on the Liverpool market has been very much curtailed in consequence of the con- tinuance of the labour troubles in Warrington and district. This naturally has resulted in an accumulation of hides, hut the number is by no means burdensome and if the dispute is pro- longed, shipments will be diverted to other places. Considering everything, values have been wonderfully steady; there has been no pressure to sell, and the feeling seems to be that unless something unforeseen occurs, higher prices for all classes of raw material must be anticipated in the autumn.

RUNCORN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Town Children in the Country.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tues- day, Mr. C. E. Linaker presiding. The Clerk read a long and interesting report of the Rural District Councils' Association's Conference, which he !and Councillor Pilling attended. There were 150 rural district coun- cils represented and a wide range of topics discussed. Councillor Pilling said the report showed them the large amount orwork the Executive Coun- cil had to do. It was ,an educational confer- enCe. Several other members spoke on the report, which was adopted.

" KINGSLEY SEWERAGE. In view of a letter from the County Medical Officer and the Clerk to the Northwich Rural Council re Ringsley sewerage, it was decided that a committee he appointed to go into the matters alleged and report to the District •Council.

STOCKTON HEATH

No Control.—Cyril lace, 81, Lythgoes-lane, Warrington, was summoned at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday for not haying proper control over a horse in Knutsford-road, Latchford Without on June 9 and was fined 3s. 6d.

On the Bench.—The magistrates present at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday were Messrs. A. H. Talbot (in the chair), J. Hepherd, P. kylmer Frost, R. C. Parr, C. Jackson Holmes, T. Baxter, W. Fletcher, and Councillor H. Roberts.

More Prizes Won.—At the Prestwich Show, first prizes in the milk and currant bread sec- tions have been won by Messrs. Hayes Bros., bakers and confectioners, London-road, Stockton Heath.-

Garden Society's Fete.—At Warrington Police Court on Tuesday Mr. J. Mclntyre, of the Red Lion Hotel, Stockton Heath, was granted a special licence to sell refreshments at the Stock- ton Heath Garden Society's fete and flower show to be held in Wilderspool Park on August Bank Holiday.

Chimney Fire.—William Lonsdale, labourer, 46, Walton-road, Stockton Heath, was summoned for allowing his chimney to be on fire on June 14. It was stated that the summons was taken out at the request of the Rural District Council of Runcorn, who had received many complaints. A fine of is., including costs was imposed.

The Smoke Nuisance.—At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday William Payne, en- gine driver, Brook-street, Krtutsford, was fined 10s. for driving a heavy engine which Was not consuming its own smoke. The police evidence showed that the engine was emitting very black smoke, and when defendant was spoken to he said he was very sorry. The chair- man (Mr. A. H. Talbot), when giving the deci- sion of the Bench, said: it was a dangerous practice to turn out a lot of smoke and sparks.

Lacked a Communication Cord.—At the Stock- ton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Robert Bond, Hawkhurst Farm, Glazebury, was sum- moned for having failed to provide a communi- cation cord between the rear wagon and the locomotive of which he was the driver. When asked to plead he replied, "Guilty, but inno- cent "—meaning he was,not aware of the re- quirements of* the by-laws. The Clerk (Mr. Greenall) If you are driving you ought to know. A fine of £l, including costs was im- posed, the chairman (Mr. A. H. Talbot) telling the defendant that he ought to be acquainted with the by-laws.

THE CHILDREN'S RAMBLE. Garden Society's Annual Picnic.

The annual picnic of the Stockton Heath Garden Society was held on Saturday afternoon. The venue waS Green-lane, and the fields in the vicinity, where the children took part in games and in a wild flower gathering competition. For the latter the youngsters were divided into three sections. The largest number of speci- mens gathered was 43. The results were as follows : Senior Section.-1, Minnie Hankey; 2, Edith Snaps. Middle Section.-1, Jennie Dunlop; 2, Nellie Douglas; 3, Muriel Mills. Junior Section.-1, Harold Churchill; 2, Mollie Harrison; 3, Vera White. Mr. Dunlop, of the Warrington Museum, was the adjudicator, and the arrangements were effectively carried out by Mr. White (the secre- tary), and the members of the committee.

IRISHMAN IN TROUBLE. Refuses to Leave Licensed Premises

and Breaks Pots.

Th 3 sequel to an Irishman's conduct in a public-house at Lower Walton on Sunday night was heard at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions an Tuesday, when John Hayden, farm labourer, of no fixed abodeovas charged with being drunk. on licensed premises and refusing to quit the Ship Inn, Lower Walton. He was further charged with breaking three mugs value 9d. Kate Rattican said, she was employed by Mr. Thomas William Griffiths at the Ship Inn. On •the day in question She saw the defendant in the house and requested him to leave, but lie- . would not-go. She Called the licengee's 'atten- tion to him, and as prisoner was going out he knocked three pint pots off the table. She valued the pots at 9d. Mr. Griffiths said the last witness called his attention to the prisoner. After the witness had asked him to leave it was some minutes before he did as requested. Witness did not see him knock the pots oft the table. Kate Rattican, recalled, said the prisoner deliberately swept them off. P.C. Richardson said he saw the prisoner leav- ing the licensed premises. The licensee was following, and • from what he stated witness stopped the prisoner and took him back to the taproom, Where the broken Pieces of the mugs were scattered over the floor,: _

When prisoner was asked if he had anything to 'say he remarked that he was willing. to pay for the damage. The Clerk (Mr. Greenall) : Is that all you have to say?—Yes, sir. How long have you been in England this last time?—Nine Weeks ' • Prisoner was fined 10s. and costs and ordered to pay for the damage. Walking on the Railway.—At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Mary Lee and Elizabeth Ann Stephenson, two women from Warrington, were summoned for trespassing on the London and North-Western Rail4-ay at Wal- ton Old Junction and exposing themselves to danger or the risk of danger.—Detective How- ard, of the railway company's police, who proved the case, stated that shunting operations were being carried on at the time. When he asked why they were on the railway they replied that they were picking coal and had seen others there.—The Clerk (Mr. Greenall) : There is a great deal of trouble down there through this sort of thing?—Yes, sir.—Stephenson, who gave a fa/se address, was fined is. 6d., and the other defendant 55., the Chairman (Mr. A. H. Talbot) remarking that if the women were brought there again the Bench would look upon it as a Very •serious offence. '

ILL-TREATING A HORSE.

Warrington Men Convicted at Stockton

Heath.

At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tues- day John ,Basford, 9, Williamson's-square, Church-street, Warrington. was charged with ill-treating a horse by working it in an unfit condition, and Charles E. Chapel, of the Howley Mortar Company, Warrington; was charged with permitting the horse to be worked. Both pleaded not guilty. Mr. C. It. Unsworth defended. Inspector Dee, of the N.S.P.C.A., said he was in London-road, Stockton Heath On June 12th, when he saw Basford in charge of two horses attached to a lurry drawing a quantity of timber. The horses had stopped on the War- rington side of London Bridge, as it was -some- what difficult to get the load over; however assistance was obtained, and as the horses got on the other side Itle noticed that the animal in question appeared to he in pain. He asked the driver what was the matter with its back, and he replied it had been a bit sore, but he thought it was all right then. At his (the inspector's) request, the saddle was removed and a sore the size of half-a-crown was found. The driver said it was all right when he turned out. He had told his master about it. When Chapel was seen he said he said he never knew the horse had a sore back, but Basford, who was passing, said he told Chapel about the horse a fortnight pre- vious. Chapel said tho drirers looked after their own horses. Inspector Breeze corroborated. Mr. Unsivorth said he admitted that the horse was in pain when seen by the inspector. His defence, however, was that before a conviction could be recorded under that section there must be a guilty knowledge in the minds of the per- s sons charged: When the horse turned out the driver saw no i gas of rawness at all. The sore was perfectly dry nu(' the horse had been work- ing the day before without giving any signs of pain, and therefore the carter was reasonably

MOORE.

Wesleyan Chapel—On Sunday two sermons .were preached by the Rev. John Gunnell, of Penketh, and collections were taken in aid of the Daresbury District Nursing Association. Good collections were taken at each service, and were in advance of last year. It was intended to hold the evening service on the lawn at the residence of Miss Dutton, but this had to be abandoned owing to the unsettled state of the weather. THE SEWERAGE SCHEME.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Progress of the Work.

At a meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday it was reported that at a meeting of the Moore Sewerage Committee, Mr. Wynne Jones (chairman) and Mr. A. de Prez (clerk of the Moore Parish Council) attended, and pressed that no further sewering of the parish be proceeded with beyond that contem- plated by the No. 1 scheme now being carried out, or in the event of its being found abso- lutely necessary to take some action with regard to the property to the east of the Daresbury Railway Station, that a very simple and a cheaper scheme be proceeded with- They also contended that the Parish Council had not been fairly treated by the District Council when the sewering of the parish was first contemplated. Upon the representatives of the Parish Council leaving the room and after discussion of their suggestions it was resolved: " That a further effort be made to acquire the land owned by Sir Gilbert Greenall as an outfall site for the No. 2 , scheme of the parish, and that the Chair- man of the committee be authorised• to point out to Mr. Jones the great saving which the acqnirement of the Site would be to the _parish, and endeavour to persuade him to use his in- fluence in assisting the Council to obtain it; that the Clerk produce, at the next meeting, a copy of the corresPondenee and other papers relating b• the action by the committee- prior to the scheme for sewering the whole of the parish being adopted; an endeavour be made to connect all properties therewith; which are situate on ' the/south side of the•Warrington to Chester rail way line; and that tlie nroposal to -extend the No. 1 Sewer across the Daresbury Station rail- way bridge be not proceeded with ;." that the branch sewer past the Post Office be diverted from the railway comnany's land in accordam‘e with a suggestion of Abe engineer, and an addi- tional manhole be constructed thereon." CoiincillorG. Pilling moved the adoption of the report, which, he said, spoke pretty well for • '

.„, Councillor Woodward mconded. " Councillor Linaker, jun.: How far ere we committed with the nrecent contractor? The Chairman (Councillor C. E. Linaker) : No further: not with regard to the No. 2 scheme. Councillor Pilling: Not in the slightest.

The Chairman : Before any remark on the attitude of the Parish Council is made I might say that this District Council has not treated the Parish Council of Vence any more unfairly than an other township.

Councillor Lister-Kaye: You were pretty hard on Gra nnenhall. The Chairman: Two different cases. The report was adopted.

FEARNHEAD.

MusicM Success.—At the London College of Music examination, held at Liverpool on Mon- day, Miss Nellie Woodhouse, of Fearnhead, passed in the first class of the senior division pianoforte playing. Miss Woodhouse, whose age is 14 years, is a pupil of Miss Atkinson, of 7, Fitzherbert-street, Warrington.

Ejectment Order Refused.—At the Warring- ton County Sessions on Wednesday Mr. George 'Mather,' rearnhead, applied for. an ejectment order against Annie Pilkington, who occupied one of his cottages in Fearnhead-lane. Mr. Arthur Browne, solioitor for the woman, said she had been a tenant of the house for 27 years.. He asked Mr. l!klathePif,be had given her notice - because she had entered a claim against him 'through her having fallen through the bedroom floor. Mr. Mather said it as not so.. She car- ried' on a fish and chip business, which he thought detrimental to the property. Mr. Browne said notice was given on the 7th July to expire on the 11th July. The house was let from Saturday to Saturday, and therefore that notice wae clearly bad.—The magistrates re- fused an ejectment order on the ground that the notice given was not sufficient.

BURTON WOOD.

A Boy's Fatal Ride.—A verdict of." Accidental death," with an expression of opinion that there ought to be a law compelling owners of motor vehicles to ha- e an-attendant on the trailer, was returned at St. Helens on Tuesday, at the in- quest on Stephen Hodgson (10), of Mercer- street; Burtonwood, who died from a fractured skull.---Evidence was given that owing to the bridge oYer the Sankey Canal being under re- pair heavy motor traffic on the Liverpool to Manchester main road has been diverted through Bold and Burtonwood. This had caused considerable excitement amongst the children of the neighbourhood, - who run up to the motor waggons and 'climb on the back in order to indulge in a free ride. On Friday of last week Hodgson tried to climb on the drawbar between the motor waggon and the trailer and fell off, being run over by the latter.—The Coroner said that the identity 'of the motor waggon had not been discovered, but he did not see that any- one was to blame, and the jury agreed with this statement.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGH LEGH.

A Pretty Wedding.—A pretty wedding was solemnised at Knutsford Parish Church on Mon- day morning, when Miss Lilian Steel, daughter of Hr. and Mrs. John Steel, of St. John's-avenue, Knutsford, became the wife o Mr. Samuel Arthur Tunstall, son of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Tunstall, of the High Legh' Post Office. The officiating minister was the Rev. B. H. Brown. The bride, who was given away by her brother, Mr. J. Steel, was adired in a neat 1)lue costume,. with black lace hat, trimmed with blue rose to match. The bridesmaid, Miss Ethel Tunstall, wore 'a crushed strawberry costume, with black hat, trimmed with roses to match, and a beauti- ful cameo brooch, the gift of the bridegroom. The duties of best man were discharged by Mr. Walter Brett. After a reception had been held at the home of the bride, Mr. and Mrs. Tunstall left for Llandudno, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling costume was of grey cloth, with a white hat. Over 50 useful and valuable presents were received.

Charge Against a Motor Cyclist—At the Lynno Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Dudley Aukland, 6, Hard-lane, St. Ifelens, was charged with driving a Motor cycle and sidecar in War- rington-road, High Legh, without exhibiting a red rear light. A letter was read from the defendant, in which he stated he had important business to attend to in London on the day the Court sat, and he would therefore be obliged, under the circumstances, if the Bench would excuse his absence. "With regard to the facts of the case," he wrote, " I beg to state that I had on my sidecar the necessary lamp showing a red rear light, which had answered satisfactorily on previous occasions. I also had attached to the motor cycle a red reflector. On this occa- sion, however, for some reason which I could not understand, I could not get the lamp to lig,ht. I had, of course, a light in Tront. I pulled up as soon as I heard the constable's whistle and explained my difficulty to him. When I reached Warrington I was able, with assistance, to put the lamp right."—P.C. Gould said he was on duty in Warrington-road, High Leh. at 10.20 p.m. on June 14th, ,when he whistled to,the defendant and drew his attention to the rear lamp, which was not alight.. Defend- ant tried to light the lamp in witness's presence, but, it woOld not act. He had a reflector on the side of his motor cycle.—The case Was adjourned until the next Court day in order to give the defeAdaat an opportunity of appearing.

LYMM. LINES FROM LYMM.

Bound Over.—At the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Emily Standish, married woman, Sandy-lane, Lynam, summoned Sarah Blagg, married woman, also of Sandy-lane, for assault. There was a cross-summons. The parties were both bound over in the sum of £5 to keep the peace for six months.

Re-considered.—At the commencement of the business at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednes- day, Mr. T. J. Ridgway said that in a case that recently came before the magistrates, John Wil- son, no settled abode, was sent to prison for 14 days' hard labour. The magistrates had re- considered their decision and he would be fined 205., including costs, or 14 days. His Memory.—When Mr. Watkin, the chair- man of the Bench, at the Petty Sessions on Wed- nesday, asked a prisoner if he had not seen him before in Court, he received the reply: "I could not say, sir; you might." (Laughter.)—The Chairman: It seems to be an annual thing. You were here about twelve months ago?—l am not sure; perhaps I was. (Laughter.)—The Chair- man: Will you promise not to come here again if we let you go?—I will, sir.—The Chairman: I suppose you will promise anything.—Prisoner was understood to say he only received a shil- ling a day whilst working on a farm at High Legh. When asked why he did not work in Lymm he replied: "They do not pay so much here."—The case was dismissed.

Deserting Husband.—At the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Florence Helen Wil- liamson, of Partington, applied for a separation order from her husband, Henry Williamson, on the grounds of desertion.—The evidence showed that defendant left home about five weeks ago, and was now living with his sister next door. Defendant was earning 225. a week and 'had given one of the children 7s. each week he had been away towards the family's maintenance. This, complainant stated, was not sufficient to keep her and the two children.—Defendant said the trouble was "all through the lodgers."— The separation order was granted, Williamson to pay Bs. 6d. per week.—Mrs. Williamson asked what it would cost to take the case to a higher Court, as she was desirous of having a stop put, to his scandalising tales. She handed a letter to the Bench, purporting to have been written by the defendant, in which he stated that his re- marks relating to his ,wife were false:—Defend- ant admitted writing it, and the Chairman (Mr. Watkin) thereupon stated that Mrs: Willianisen could leave the Court -Without a stain on her character. .

WOMAN. ON FIRE. Sequel to a Neighbours Difference • Which Has Lasted for Years.

At the Lymm Petty Sessions on 'Wednesday' morning Caroline Shepherd summoned Bessie Jones for assauit. Both are married and are neighbours living next door to •one another in Bridgewater-street, Lymm. There was a cross- summons for assault. Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for Mrs. Shepherd and Mr. T, Ridgway appeared for Mrs. Jones. Mr. Steel stated that Mrs. Shepherd carried on the business of a tobacconist, whilst the hus- band of Mrs. Jones was a hardware dotstler. He understood that there had been unpleasantness for some years, and the parties had not been on speaking terms for over two years. On the 24th June Mrs. Shepherd had occasion to go into the pass.age at the back of the shops, and whilst she was there she saw that Mrs. Jones had some rubbish in a basket, which, after being set on fire, she placed near Mrs. Shep- herd's yard gate. Mrs. Shepherd, who was 67 years of age, called Mrs. Jones "a red vixen," whereupon Mrs. Jones struck the old lady vio- lently on the head with a yard brush, which she had been using to stir up the fire. The brush was burning, and this act set on fire Mrs. Shepherd's blouse, burned her underclothing, and injured the skin. "YOU HAVE SET ME ON FIRE." She was on fire and shouted: 'Oh, Mrs. Jones, you have set me on fire," but Mrs. Jones de- clined to put it out, and had it not been for a Mrs. Sutton seeing the woman from a bedroom window and rushing out to extinguish the fire, the injuries might have been very serious. Mrs. Jones, instead of putting out the fire, brought her husband, who said: "I saw you strike my wife," and Mrs. Shepherd replied: "Oh, you liar !" The old lady went to see Dr: Burrows, but he was out, and the' next day when she called he dressed her injuries. • ' 1 Mr. Steel suggested that the assault was a serious one, having regard to the age of the complainant. Mrs. Shepherd had also suffered from shock. He would like to point out another circumstance. Mrs. Shepherd issued her sum- mons the day following the assault, and the magistrates would notice that a cross-suminons had now been issued by Mrs. Jones. That was not issued until July Bth. So far as Mrs. Shepherd was concerned, she had no desire that there should be a heavy penalty, but in conse- quence of things that had taken place in the past, she would like to have Mrs. Jones bound over so that she might be secure from any assault. or any molestation in the future. INJURED WOMAN'S STORY.

_ Mrs. Shepherd, in giving evidence, bore out her solicitor's statement. Cross-examined, she, denied that she was also burning paper in her yard. She did not see any paper roll from Mrs. Jones's pile. Mr. Ridgway: My story is that when Mrs. Jones went to laush up a piece of burning paper, you rushed at her with your brush and assaulted her. Witness (excitedly) : Nothing at all of the sort; it's audaciously untrue. Is it a fact that Mr. and Mrs. Jones have had to complain about you in the past?—l , know nothing of that. Is it not a fact that you go in your yard and burn paper while she is washing?—No, sir. I scorn to do it. _l.rs. Jones refused to put the fire out?—Yes; Mrs. Sutton had to do it. Mr. Steel: You have been asked about this unpleasantness; now tell us how long it has been going on. • Witness: One Bank Holiday after I had been a walk I returned to find the key-hole had been stuffed up, and I had to pick it out with a hat- pin. I then placed it in the Jones' key-hole, becausel knew they had done it. Questioned, she said she did not see the put it in the key-hole, but they were at the door when she went out. Reference was made by• Mrs. Shepherd to a well-known Warrington man wishing to have a shed to store his goods in, but Mr. Shepherd refused to let it, saying he had a neighbour in the same business. Later the Warrington man opened in another part of the village, and the Joneses had blamed them for it. In reply to the Chairman (Mr. Watkin), she said she had no brush in her hand. Mrs. Sutton, a married woman, of Waste-lane, Thelwall, gave evidence. She did not see the assault. Witness put the fire out; Mrs. Jones did not put it out.

Questioned by the Hon. J. E. Cross, witness said the clothing was alight. Mr. Steel produced the clothing, showing traces of the burning. THE JONESES' VERSION. • - • •

Mr. Ridgway, at the commencement of his address, said that the reason why Mrs. Jones had not issued a summons earlier was because she had been confined to bed for some days. When the" incident' took place Mr. Jones said he would communicate with his solicitor, and Probably that was why Mrs. Shepherd had been SO soon. Mrs. Jones had been attended by Dr. Leorrard Thorpe, and it was only as soon as she was discharged from bed that she went to War- rington and saw him (Mr. Ridgway). . His story was an entire denial. On June 24th both Mrs. Jones and Mrs. Shepherd were burning paper, when a piece rolled from Mrs. Jones' pile to- wards Mrs. Shepherd's premises. Mrs. Jones was brushing this back, when Mrs. Shepherd came out, called her a vixen, and commenceil beating her with a brush. Mr. Jones, on look- ing through the window, saw Mrs. Shepherd with her brush raised as if to strike his wife, and he rushed out. Mrs. Jones did put the fire out by pinching the burilllng part , with her finger and thumb. Defendant's Version.

Mrs. Jones ec;;;;(3.1;;;;:te'd liar solicitor's state- ment.

Cross-examined. she said that on the day fol- lowing the incident she walked to Stockton Heath and back, but it was with pain. Do you make out you are an angel ?-=-No. And that Mrs. Shepherd is the other person?

Mrs. Jones contended that Mrs. Shepherd set herself on fire when she raised her brush to strike witness, for probably a piece of burning material fell on her back from the brush.

Mr. Jones, the husband of the previous wit-

ness, said his wife put the fire out in his pre- sence by pinching it with her thumb and finger. Arising out of a question put by Mr. Steel, Mr. Ridgway stated he had a letter from Dr Thorpe, but Mr. Steel objected to it being put in, saying he also had one from Dr. Burrows, but it was not right to hand. them in, as they could not cross-examine the doctors' statements.

CASES DISMISSED.

After a brief retirement, the Chairman said the Bench were sorry they had not the doctors' evidence, for that might probably have enabled them to come to a conclusion. However, from the evidence befo're them, they thought it was six of one and half a dozen of the' other, and tne cases would be dismissed, both partits pay- ing their costs.

OUGIITRINGTON'S NEW RECTOR.

Mid-Cheshire Nicar's Preferment.

It has been announced that the Rev. G. S. Tudor Evans, vicar of Lostock 'Gralam, near Northwich, has accepted the rectorship of Ought- rington, made vacant by the cession of the Rev. E. T. Wethered. Mr. Evans was ordained deacon in 1892, and priest in the following year. He was a curate at Witton, Northwich, from 1894 until 1902, when he was appointed vicar of Los- tock Gralam, a position he has filled until now. At a special meeting of his parishioners on Monday he informed them that the living of Oughtrington had been offered to him by the Bishop of Clxester, and that he had that day accepted. _

The parishioners exproksed their sorrow at his departure, but wished him "God speed" in the duties he would shortly be taking up. Whilst Mr. Evans has been in Lostock he has proved his worth as an organiser; this is indi- cated by the healthy condition of the various parochial undertakings and by the fact that he has been instrumental in raising large sums of money on behalf of ,the parish church and schools.

Amongst the good work he has done in the parish was the establishment some years ago of the men's Bible class, which now has about 80 members on its books. Recently the members of tlie class were responsible for extending a building in the vicarage grounds which is used as their meeting hall and also as a social club. As a preacher, Mr. Evans is well known, for his discourses are always forceful and to the

Although it will be a difficult matter to suc- ceed a rector so popular as Mr. Wethered was, it is certain that Mr. Evans will not be found wanting in any branch of church and parochial work.

Thrown from His Cart.—Owing to, his horse becoming suddenly restive when passing the Dam Head on Saturday, Thomas Hughes, 4, Lythgoes-lane, Warrington, a carter employed by Messrs. Henry Fairclough and Co., of War- rington, was thrown from his cart and rendered unconscious. He was found by several cyclists, who gave every assistance; and afterwards he was removed to his lodgings at Lymm Bridge, where Dr. Burrows attended to his injuries. Later he was taken to the Warrington Infir- mary, and is reported to ,be making satisfactory progress.

_ MAY QUEEN.

Children Entertained to Tea and Sports A party of three hundred children thoroughly enjoyed themselves at the C.T.C. Farm on We.d- nesday evening, when they were entertained to a substantial tea, followed ny sports and games. T,Jiese little guests were made welcome by tile May Queen Festival Committee—" The Hustlers," as they are called in the village—in return for the help the children gave on " the one and only" great day in the village. Each child had taken a part in the procession, which won golden opinions on the day of the festival. At the tea it was found necessary to have two sittings, and the wants of the girls were at- tended to first, and, immediately they had fin- ished, the'boys showed that the waiting had not impaired their appetites, for they seemed to enjoy the good things supplied by Mr. J. B. Egerton, the new proprietor of the farm. The children, when in the field, enjoyed them- selves to the full, and at the conclusion of the sports:Mrs. James Webb, jun., presented the prizes to the winners. Scrambling for sweets and oranges proved a pleaSurable time for the children. Mr. Wilcock, the landlord of the Fleece Hotel, was responsible for a good deal of fun, as he was accompanied by a large quan- tity of sweets and oranges, which were soon distributed.

THE PRIZE-WINNERS. The following were the various events and the winners :

50 Yards Flat Race (boys under eight years).- 1; J. Higgins; 2, T. Greenhalgh. 50 Yards Flat Race (girls under eight years).- 1, L. Moston ; 2, V. Kitchen; 3, M. Leah. 100 Yards Flat Race (boys between 8 and 12).- 1, S. Owen ;,2, R. Bate.

75 Yards Flat Race (girls between 8 and 12).- 1, E. Warham; 2, F. Whitelegg. 100 Yards Flat Race (boys 12 and over).-1, J. Morris; 2, H. Kinsey. 100 Yards Flat Race (girls 12 and over).-1, M. Walker; 2, M. Jolley. 50 Yards Wheelbarrow Race (boys 10 years and over).-1, T. Shaw and A. Kerfoot; 2, S. Jackson and J. Fogg. 50 Yards Skipping-rope Race (girls 10 and over).-1, H. Wilson; 2, F. Leah. 50 Yards Three-legged Race (boys 8 to 12).-1, 11. Owen and R. Atherton; 2, F. Jackson and J. Smith.

50 Yards Three-legged Race (girls 8 to 12).-1, M. Hewitt and E. Lewis; 2, W. Lawrenson and S. Skelhorn. 50 Yards Three-legged Race (Ladies' Commit- tee).—l, Miss Pearson and Miss W. Mairs; 2, Mrs Webb and Mrs. Hind. , The prizes in the Ladies' Committee race were kindly given by Mr. Hudson, the chairman, and an additional prize from Mr. P. Cooke, a mem- ber of the old committee, was divided amongst those under five years of age, namely, T. Hig- gins, E. Higgins, G. Eckleston, M. Inglefield, and L. Leah. After the presentation of the prizes Mr. J. H. Warham said they all knew how near death the festival had been, and how, in a very short time, the committee had got the festival ready. He was sure it was only by the united efforts of a hard-working committee that the festival had been a success. Special thanks were due to the ladies, who had done all they could for the event. Applause.)
