PENKETH.

SCHOOL PRIZE PRESENTATION.

Features of the Friends' School.

The annual 'general meeting and prize distri- bution of the Penketh Friends School took place at the school on Thursday. A House Committee was appointed in the morning and inspected the living quarters of the scholars, Mrs. George A. Pickard voicing satisfaction at the general neat- ness and cleanliness of the rooms at the general meeting in the afternoon. Lessons dealing with a wide range of subjects were afterwards given, together with exhibi- tions of a variety of work in the various class- rooms.

The business meeting was held in the Lecture Hall, Mr. George A. Pickard occupying the chair. There were also on the platform: Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes (treasurer), Samuel B. Lester (of Liverpool, a member of the Com- mittee of Management), and W. E. Brown (head- master).

===== 0002601_19140718_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
COMMITTEE'S REPORT.

The TBth annual report of the Committee Of Management was read, and stated that the num- ber of children in the school continued to in- crease, the final term of the year having opened with the largest number on record, viz., 96. Of these, 35 were members of the Society of Friends, 29 were connected with it, and 31—including 10 day scholars—were unconnected with it. This gratifying increase had taken place in spite of a growing number of children using Penketh merely as a preparatory stage in their education before passing on elsewhere. Hardly more than one-third of the pupils completed their school career with them, and the consequent prema- ture loss of many promising pupils was one of the difficulties with which they had to contend. Nevertheless, continued the report, the stand- ard of scholastic attainment appeared to have surpassed the level of previous years, as was shown by the number of successes in external examinations. The school had gained four passes in the Senior College of Preceptors' ex- amination, one boy being awarded the " Taylor- Jones" prize for the highest Scripture marks; a pass in the higher division of the school exam— ination of the Associated Board of the Royal Academy of Music and the Royal College of Music; four candidates placed in the honours lists of the Incorporated Society of Musicians; two transference scholarships to Ackwortli from the Central Education Committee; and three of the first four places in the recent Friends' Peace Society essay competition: _ _ _

" lemay 1)e trufy said," the report concludes, "that a spirit of earnest work, an improving standard of conduct, and a highly satisfactory tone pervades our children, and also that an encouraging feeling of unity and of corporate effort is manifested in the general life of the institution." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140718_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
HEADMASTER'S REPORT.

In the course of his report, the Headmaster expressed his pleasure at the receipt of a third donation of .15, which would enable four boys to attend the Free Church CamP at Matlock in the holidays. There was no need to say much regarding the new buildings—they spoke eloquently for the labours of all who had been concerned with their design .and care. _ . _

The high number of pupils in the school was maintained. Last term there had been 89, and this term 95, although those numbers did not reach the limit of the school's accommodation.

_ They continued to suffer through losing chil- dren at an early age, but there were signs of a general lengthening of the years spent at school. During the past three years 93 children had entered the school and 80 left. In the tabular statement relating to the eight Friends' Schools which reported to the yearly meeting, Penketh stood first in excess of income over expenditure, as well as in annual subscriptions and donations.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
A Sound Education.

The school had recently been inspected by Mr. C. J. R. Tipper, Director of Education for the County of Westmorland, who considered the buildings and equipment to be generally ade- quate and convenient. The school was giving a sound education on good lines, an excellent moral training, and was providing for the healthy, physical development of its pup.ls. 1 he headmaster and his staff were to be congratu- lated. (Applause.) The Chairman said that those present joined with the examiner, in that congratulation. Messrs. Arnold Hodgkinson and C. Jackson Holmes were unanimously re-elected secretary and treasurer respectively for the ensuing year. The committee was requested to consider the necessity of the appointments of additional trustees, the number being at present reduced to four.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

After tea had been partaken of in the dining hall the prize distribution and entertainment were held in the lecture hall. The Ileadmasmr occupied the chair.

Mr. Ueorge A. Pickard said that the scholars, whilst at school, learnt to take an interest in all kinds of things, and to pursue various hob- bies. He wanted them to continue to take an interest, .after leaving school, in those tita,gs which they had liked whilst at school. In after- life, the man who had a hobby, or subject in which he was interested, could become an ex- pert in that branch of knowledge. For instance, drawing was a most interesting and va:uabie hobby; and it was not the only one. • The speaker then proceeded to describe how many of the school's subjects migitt hc developed by the scholars in after-life, and made profit- able. Moreover, they never lost their interest, because nobody ever knew" everything that ccas to be known about them. (Applause.) The Headmaster thanked Mr. Pickard for his interesting and instructive address, and referred to his own hobby experiences. Mr.' Pickard then proceeded to distribute the study and sports prizes and certificates, of w there was a large number. Walton i. proposed, in a neat speech, and Ethel Eckersley seconded, a vote of thanks to Mr. Pickard for so kindly coming to distribute the prizes and to encourage them. (Applause.) An excellent entertainment followed, which consisted of several musical selections and reci- tations in French and English.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
ROSES AND SWEET PEAS.

• First Show of the Season. , The Penketh, Sankey, and District Rose and *Sweet Pea Society held their sixth annual exhi- bition at Bank House Farm on Saturday. The Chairman (Mr. W. Woods) introduced this year's President (Mrs. C. E. Parker) to the members and friends of the society. The President said that it had given her great pleasure to be chosen president of this society, for she felt that the growing of roses was a great benefit to the village at large. She was con- vinced that they had not only a beautifying, but an elevating effect upon the man who grew them. To quote Dean Hole: "The man who grew roses to perfection must grow them in his heart first," which meant that he must love ,tli4,n for them- selves. Just as a child, however well fed. clothed and educated, was never so perfect with- out love, so the man who grew roses solely for the sake of profit naturally failed to get the beet out of them.

The Rev. J. R. Jones, in mo.-in a vote ci thanks, said that it was 24 years ;Igo sin, e the President's mother (the late Mrs. Garnett) and himself opened the first show•in Penketh. TABLE TALK,

The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News. .

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

Why should we import tea from China and India and coffee from Brazil when our own meadows, roadsides and gardens contain aro- ivatic plants that could be made to give us just as delicious beverages? asked Dr. Bouquet in an article in the Paris "Temps." He sug- gests the common vervain and the bee-balm as examples. Others of which our ancestors used to make infusions are orange blossoms, the buds of, lime trees, black currant leaves, and mint.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
Water Sold as Pork.

A certain butcher: who sold about thirty cooked legs of pork per week; said his loss was formerly 40 per cent., and is now only 22 per cent. in the electric oven, or a clear saving of 18 per cent After deducting the cost of cur- rent, his saving amounted to 2s. per leg of pork, or .83 per week extra profit. On being told that he only saved water; he answered: " Perhaps so; but, you see, my customers pay me Is. 2d. per pound for it!"

===== 0002601_19140718_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Potato Juice for Curing Pains.

Potato juice as a remedy for sprains, lum- bago, gout, rheumatism and bruises is recom- mended by Dr. Heaton C. Howard, of London,'l in au article in the "Lancet." He cites num- erous cases in his own practice in which the pain has been relieved quickly, sometimes by the first -application, and the fluid that has exuded into the joint or the membranes has been absoAed within a few days. It is often used for burns. Potato juice is used as an ointment, a liniment, or a plaster.

' A New Australian Nut. What is described in an American paper as the best flavoured nut in the world has been discovered in the Macadamia Ternifolia, or Queensland nut, to which some publicity is given in the 'Melbourne Press." The nut is a hardy evergreen, a native of 'Queensland and New South Wales, is perfectly round, about an inch in diameter, and of a yellow colour. The kernel is solid, having no divisions, covered with a thick skin, and entirely fills the shell. The texture resembles the Barcelona nut, and the flavour is distinctly pleasant. It has a very hard shell, though the hardness .is said to vary with different localities. It is said to compare very favourably with any other edible nut.

Labour Papers in Germany. In the "London Typographical Journal" for June there is an article upon trade unions in Berlin, from which it can be seen how, in some respects, the Labour movement in Germany differs from that in England. This is most noticeable in the use made of the Press. In Germany there are ninety daily Labour news- papers, all self-supporting, and eighty-two of these are printed and issued from buildings owned by the Labour party. Of these papers " Vorwarts", has a circulation of 170,000. It appears that in Germany the papers issued by the various wings of the Labour movement leave all hostile criticism of each other to the enemy.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
America's Future Language.

The English use slang, and inow- that they are using it. The Americans (says a writer in the "Academy") use slang and think they are using English, because their slang, to a great extent, is the language of their business; and the American,. first, last, and always, is a business man. It is therefore quite true to say that American slang, as we know it in England, is the language of America to a far greater extent than English slang, as it is known in America, is the language of England. In ten years' time I think that there will be very little English left* in America. There will be nothing but slang—the commercial jar- gon of the office and the store.

Whitewash as Good as Paint. The following is the recipe given by "The Smallholder" for a new whitewash which will withstand the weather almost as well as paint, and is therefore suitable for outside work : Slake in boiling water half a bushel of lime, strain it so as .to remove all sediment, and add two pounds of sulphate of zinc, one pound of common salt, and half a pound of *biting, thoroughly dissolved. Mix the whole to a proper consistency with skim milk, and apply hot. Any colouring matter may be added. Those who have used it claim that it will compare very favourably with lead paints, both in appearance and durability.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
To Heat Water in Ten Seconds.

Rere ia lie rus i in—e-ans of which a glass of water can be heated in ten seconds, and boiled in less than a minute. It is the invention of a Frenchman. A metal cylinder is fastened to a wooden handle from which pro- jects a wire ending in a plug that can be in- serted in any electric light socket. When the current is turned on, the electricity heats the metal cylinder and this heats the water. The same apparatus can be used for deodorising or perfuming the air of a room, a pastille of in- cense, lavender flowers, or any other similar substance being put into the cylinder •before the current is turned on. It is a convenient article for travellers, who often want a little hot water in a hotel where none is immediately available, but where there is electric light. Any electric bulb can be unscrewed, the socket of the electric heater inserted in its place and the current turned on.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
Stutterers Talk Too Fast.

Dr. Niara,ge, of Paris, told the Academie des Sciences recently that stuttering—which is an intermittent nervous condition of all the sound-producing organs, lungs, larynx and mouth—can be cured by making the victim understand his affliction and the means by which he can rid himself of it. Dr. Marage showed moving pictures of the mouths of nor- mal and stuttering persons and of the vjbra- tions of their voices. These show that stut- terers speak their words three times as rapidly as normal persons, that the Words are sebar- ated by intervals only half as long, and that there is no division of syllables. The stutterer talks rapidly because he breathes badly, and is always out of breath. Therefore, the first treatment for him is to teach him to breathe correctly.

Hollow Steel Bars: Novel Scheme. A recent discovery about the production of hollow steel bars promises much, says "Science Siftings." Such bars have important advan- tages for many mechanical purposes, but have been expensive to manufacture by methods re- quiring drilling. In the new process, bars of thrce-fourths inch to three inches in diameter and lengths up to 20 feet are made from drilled billets. The hole in the billet is packed with a dense heat-resisting sand, closed tightly at the ends with metal plugs, and the billet is then heated, and repeatedly rolled until broUght to the desired size. An approximately round hole, bearing about the original relation in size to the entire mass,, is maintained through the bar. The hollow bars are being adopted for a great variety of purposes.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 18, 1914.

Literary Gleanings.

HONOLULU. The first impressiol of Honolulu was inter- esting. It is hardly like a city when the strictly business section is passed, but more like a suc- cession of country estates, bordering palm- shaded avenues, with brilliant yellow ,begonia vines spilling over the walls, and huge bougain- villea trees, like giant magenta rhododendrons, leaning over the gates. One 'gets glimpses of sloping green lawns, intersected by meandering rills, dotted with clumps of papaia and guava trees, and bordered by hedges of the glowing red hibiscus, flower. The climate of Honolulu seems to be ideal; it maintains a soft even tempera- ture the year round, never hot and never cold, not varying more than 15 degrees winter and summer.— It is swept constantly by delicious breezes of the trade winds, and rains fall almost every day, but they are generally slight showers that refresh without causing satiety. During our two days' stay we were enter- tained every moment of the time, and the spon- taneous unaffected hospitality reminded one of the plantation days in the old South. Our hostess was a charming lady and told us many interesting tales. She was descended from one .of the early families who now constitute the aristocracy of the place, and she had, so she told us, in illustration of the large patriarchal fami- lies, fifty-four first cousins in and around Hono- lulu. She told.us a good deal about the natives, and especially the chiefs, who seem to be of different breed entirely from the commons, and have never inter-married with them; the com- mons .could not formerly even touch a chief, on pain 'of death. The chiefs are fed with special food, reminding one of a queen in a hive, and are bigger, stronger, and more intelligent than the others. She told us she believed the islands to be the topmost peaks of a sunken continent. The Hawaiians are wonderful swimmers, and we spent an interesting hour at Waikiki Beach, watching their water sports. The board- swimming required great skill; each fellow lies on a board, face downward, and swims out be- yond the surf line, where he stands upright on the board and rides the breakers back to land. Some would stand on their heads on the boards, some dived from them and cut many antics; it takes months of practice and skill to be able to ride the boards at all. It is an old Hawaiian custom that had alinost died out, and was res- cued just in time. Some of the young white men are as proficient as their dark companions. The surf-boats too are famous here —From " The Theosophical Path.'"

===== 0002601_19140718_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PIPE ORGAN.

. The organ-pipe is an overgrown penny whistle. From the huge "Double Open Diapason" pipes, as they are called, the largest of which would make a very respectable factory smoke-stack, to the tiny little pipe whose voice is so high and faint and shrill it seems unnecessary to place it in the organ, the "flue work" of an organ are but scientifically-made whistles. But what a variety of whistles is here! Pipes are round, square, triangular. They are of wood, of differ- ent kinds of metal, open at the top, closed at the top, or half-open and half-closed, with one mouth, with two mouths: some with reeds and some without, long, short, fat, thin, in endless variety.

Let no one think of an organ only as a collec- tion of huge whistles, with a mechanism for blowing them at will. For this huge house of sounds, is constructed according to the most abstruse and scientific mathematical laws, and, stranger yet, some of the laws no man under- stands. Grave scientists will wrangle about what makes an organ-pipe speak, and as to why a certain pipe gives a certain tone when another pipe, precisely like it save for a minute differ- ence in the shape of the mouth of the pipe, gives a totally different tone.

Take an open- PI/;;- eight feet long, which speaks a certain low, grave note. Put a top on the pipe, and the note immediately descends almost a full octave. Take a pipe of certain length and cut a hole in it; the note ascends. The organ is full of such peculiarities and con- tradictions. For instance, no organ was ever yet wholly and entirely in tune. Temperature is the main reason. Tune an organ at 70 degrees temperature, raise or lower the temperature one degree, and different pipes are affected in a dif- ferent degree. Draw the stop on an organ labelled "Mixture, three ranks," and press any single key. Not a single note, but a beautifully flute-throated chord of three notes answers.

Press two keys or three keys which ordinarily would form a chord in harmony. The result is a squeal beside which that of a pig under a gate is music itself. Now draw the stop called "Open Diapason" and depress the keys of your chord. Although the same pig squeal is sound- ing, it is no longer beard as a squeal, but as a deep, beautiful, rich note. ' The resonance is most curious and most inter- esting. When a violin how is drawn across the string we bear a certain note—let us suppose it is the D string which is being sounded, and we bear the note of D. With that fundamental note of D sounds also the octave note, then still another note which is twelve tones above, again another note fifteen tones above, another seven- teen tones above, and so on, until the sounds of these "harmonic tones," as they are called, die out in altitude too great for human ears.—C. Claudy, in "The Girl's Own Paper and Woman's Magazine" for July.

NEW BENEFIT UNDER INSURANCE ACT.

Life Pensions If Disabled.

The clause of the National Insurance Act relating to disablement benefit came into force on Monday. It provides that in cases of permanent dis- ablement or incapacity arising from illness the injured person will qualify at the end of the tfi -weeks' period for which he receives the sick ay for a special weekly grant of :"s. until the age of 70 is reached, when the disablement benefit is superseded by the old-age pension. ' The amount of the benefit is the same for men and women, and it is a condition of its payment that 104 full weeks' contributions must have been paid in respect of the claimant before the benefit is paid. The present week is the earliest possible date of qualification, and in respect of insured per- sons who had received 26 weeks' full benefit up to Saturday and are not in arrears on the two years the disablement benefit becomes payable frotn to-day. • •

MUNICIPAL GOLF.

The Question of the Cost not a Serious Ohs=lle to the Workers.

NO ELABORATE CLUBS REQUIRED.

" EXAMINER " SPECIAL

When the scheme to provide a _muincipal golf course has come to fruition, Warrington will be placed on an equal footing with many other cities and towns throughout the country. Everywhere that Councils have provided courses success has crowned their efforts; indeed some towns could be named which make goodly sums annually out of their enterprise. Golf for the workers, some people think, is not feasible. They argue that the workers cannot afford the cost of providing the imple- ments. They further contend that golf is too costly a pastime. Looked at from the basis of club golf this is true enough. Clubs are very exclusive and the entrance fee and annual sub- scription are high. It is the privilege of belong- ing to a club that mounts the cost up, and then with a private club the cost of laying out and maintaining the course is a serious item. If the municipality provide the course golf is im- mediately brought within reach of the purse of the artisan. This is what the Warrington Town Cg, and they can do it aso uhnacvi contemplatese eresef eth edio. i towns.n

===== 0002601_19140718_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ACQUIRED TASTE.

The playing of golf, like the eating of toma- toes, is an acquired taste, and once the workers take to the, pastime few make better exponents of the game. This has: been proved on many a municipal course, more particularly in Scot- land where Such courses have been in use for many years. What artisans in other towns have achieved: those of Warrington can also do. Warringtonians have a reputation as "sports." To give one example we need only refer to the case of bowling. This; ,though in itself an ancient game, is of quite recent intro- duction into Warrington, and it has obtained a tremendous hold upon all grades of workers as is made evident, every Saturday during the season with its long array of matches. and mains. According to one bowling official Warrington for its population has a greater army of bow- lers than most towns in the country.

THE COST OF GOLF. What has happened in the case of bowling may probably be repeated in the case of golf once the Warrington Council provide facilities for the workers participating in the "Royal and Ancient Game." It may not be long, before Warrington can present a strong group of work- ing men golfers, some of whom. may yet—and one hardly thinks this an idle dream—be seen figuring in amateur championships. Golf is more costly, especially to get initiated into the game, than bowls. Clubs and balls are required, and these are undoubtedly more costly than a pair of bowls. But an artisan golfer need not begin with a bag full of " sticks" like those possessed by the more wealthy exponents. A good driver, cleek, and putter are sufficient to enable one to play a good game over any course. These can be had for about 7s. 6d. each. Balls cost from 2s. to 2s. 6d. each, and these are a heavy item, as the " wee white ball" has a very bad habit of getting lost. Thus for about ;30s. a player can be quite respectably equipped.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
CHARGE ON .PUBLIC COURSES.

At the moment of writing nothing has been mooted in regard to the charge for playing on our future municipal course: There is no likeli- hood that this will be extortionate. The custo- mary charge all over the country is 2d. per round of eighteen holes, although there are cases where 6d. is the charge, but such rates only prevail at such Meccas of golf as St. An- drew's and North Berwick. Edinburgh, the pioneer of municipal golf, has a universal charge of 2d. for play on the long course on the Braid Hills, and the nine- hole course on the same hills is free, with the exception of a few days in the year. Glasgow makes the same charge; and at Heaton Park, Manchester, a similar tarifi,..prevails, and through these twopences sufficient money is raised to keep the course in the best condition.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
A FASCINATING GAME.

Golf is as fascinating as it is an ancient game. To the uninitiated it seems an absurdity strik- ing a little ball and then following it up to mameuvre it into a hole. To others the trund- ling of a brace of bowls up and down a green in the wake 'of a "jacks' may seem equally—as silly, but both have their fine points, and if anything golf in this respect beats bowls. There are many ways in which interest is con- tinuously maintained in the game of golf. There are numerous forms of competitions, it can be played by two players, or as a foursome, in which four players, two aside, play with a ball for each couple, each partner dealing with the ball in turn. In connection with many muni- cipal courses shields and cups are offered for competition, either individually or as teams. In a word, the varieties in the game are almost endless, and so the working man golfer finds when he takes up the pastime. AN INTERESTING HISTORY. The history of municipal golf is interesting. At one time golf was practically a wealthy man's game. Kings and courtiers, centuries ago, Played it at St. Andrew's, Leith, or Mussel- burgh. Some 35 years ago Edinburgh Council Purchased a range of hills on its outskirts known as the Braid Hills. One artisan golfer started an agitation through the Press in favour of a por- tion of the hills being laid out as a golf course for the use of the workers. At first he was the butt of mirth. Knowing people joked about " tanner " or sixpenny golf, but the tirtisan in the end lived down the oppo- sition and a course was laid out. The workers entered into the game with zest, and soon their numbers so increased that the course had to be extended. Within the last ten years it has been still further extended and a second course has been laid out. That long range of hills is now entirely given over to the use of the golfers, and Edinburgh has found, at a charge of 2d. per round, a little gold mine. Other towns have had similar experiences with their municipal golf links. They all pay and are a great boon to the workers. Probably Warrington may have a similar experience.

CHILDREN'S LEAGUE OF PITY.

A Successful Garden Sale.

The promoters of the local branch ' of the Children's League of Pity, a League organised by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, held a most successful gar- den sale on Saturday at Kelmscott, Appleton, by kind permission of Mrs. Segar Owen. The work of the League was commenced 27 years ago, and has since proved a valuable asset to War- rington life under the guidance of Miss Con- stance Broadbent, as secretary, and Inspector Wainwright. The many attractions provided besides the sale included character-reading, photography, and a dramatic performance, while the children enjoyed various garden games. Mrs. Frank Bush, of the Central Office, Lon- II don, who is one of the League organisers, gave the children' a short address upon the good work they were assisting by means of their contribu- tion cards, and cited several pathetic cases of in- fant neglect whieh had been brought to the League's attention at different times. Members of the League never begged, but there were other kinds of aid besides that of finance, and cloth- ing and toys were always welcomed by the local secretaries. Thanks were due to Mrs. Segal-, Owen and Miss Broadbent for their assistance that afternoon. (Applause.) After kiitably replying, Miss Broadbent, who presided, thanked Mrs. Bush for her instructive address. ! An interesting programme which followed in- , eluded songs by Miss Adams and Miss M. Mars- den, of Burnley, a duologue, "Shattered Nerves," by Mrs. Watts and Miss Dorothy Garfit; and a humorous piano sketch by Mr. C. H. Clarke. Miss Gladys Adams accompanied the songs on the piano. Tea was afterwards provided. There was a large attendance, and as a result of the day's effort £26 was 'contributed to the fuude a the League.

TAKEN IN.

The usual crowd of small boys was gathered about the entrance of circus tent in a small town one day, pushing and trying to get a glimpse of the interior. A man standing near watched tl)em for a few moments; then walking up to the ticket-taker he said with an air of authority:—

"Let all these boys in, and count them as they pass." The gateman did as requested, and when the last one had gone he turned and said, "Twenty- eight, sir."

" Good," said the man, smiling as he walked away, "I thought I guessed right."

, SHE FORGOt HERSELF.

Pauline, who had been attending school for two weeks, was telling her parents about the naughtiness of her classmates. "That's bad," said her mother, "and did the teacher have to speak to you, too?" "No," said Pauline; "site had to speak to all the class but me this afternoon." "Good," replied her mother. "What dii she say?" "Why," answered l'auline, "she said—' Nory, children, we will all Wait until Pauline is in order.'"

===== 0002601_19140718_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WILL AND THE WAY.

The path of the pedestrian through • the Caucasus and along' the Black Sea is by no means easy. An anecdote by Mr. Stephen Graham, that appears in "Changing Russia," illustrates one of the many difficulties of such a trip. Meeting an aged peasant driving an cur team Mr. Graham asked where the highroad lay. " There is no road," said he. I thought the answer to be stupidity, so I asked him in which direction Otchemehiri lay. By the last milestone it was only eight versts distant. I could do that without a• road if I could be sure of the direction. The man led me to his cottage, climbed on the roof, and bade me follow. Then he pointed out the direction.

What was my astonishment to see at a short distance a river as wide as the Thames, hurry- on to the sea.

"And where is the bridge?" I asked. "I don't understand." "Bridge, the way over." " Oh, the way over—there is none." "Can I wade across?" "No." . "Is it possible to swim?" • "The current is striang." "How do you go to Otcheinchiri?" "Don't go," "What! Have you never been to Otchem- chin F" "Never." "But it is only five miles." ' The old man gruffly beckoned me to come down from the roof and stop babbling. "And how do other people come across?" "They don't come." "How long have you lived here?" , • "Sixty-four years.' "But surely in sixty-four years some people have crossed to Otchemchiri? My map shows an unbroken chausee, a 'division one' road." My map made no impression on the old fel- low. He pointed to the inside of his house, and indicated that I could spend the night there with the pig and the chickens, if I liked. As for people coming over, there used to he a wooden way, hut it was washed out to sea long ago.

-- I saw be did not know the Russian word for bridge, and that his wooden way was the old bridge. I asked where this had been.

My would-be host was not offended by my pertinacity, an he told me quite explicitly the way to where the bridge had been.

Alas! it was as he said. I found all the foundations of a substantial wooden structure, but no cross-beams—nothing on which even an acrobat could have made his way across. I walked disconsolately along the shore. It was a fine, broad river, flooded by the rain, shallow at the shore, hut swiftly flowing over the stones. Yet even half-way across were great stones not covered by water. It was almost possible to step from stone to stone, to the main current. I wondered whether, after all, it were very deep. As I was speculating, I came across a deep cart rut, and I saw at a glance that, at any rate in normal weather, carts found a fording placelhere. I resolved to try what could he done. I un- dressed and stuffed my clothes into my capa- cious sack, strapped it over my shoulders, and started on thy cold, unpleasant, absurd adven- ture. I waded where the current of the stream was broken by stones, and after a hundred yards I was only just above the knee in water. Here, however, the channel suddenly deepened. The water, coming straight from the now- covered hills, was atrociously cold; and at the point where I suddenly went up to the waist in it, I nearly resolved to turn back to the shore and spend the night with the aged man. Yet, , gasping and shivering, I followed the lines of ripples that showed the shallowest Places, came once more into the shallows, went into depths again, and again into shallows, and reached at last the more solid slope of the farther shore. The current, although not powerful, was diffi- cult'to withstand, and at every step it threat- ened to bowl me over. '

One hour later I had walked some fresh warmth into my veins after the icy dill of the river, and I came to Otchenachiri, found its Hotel France, and ordered the best hot dinner. they could give.

AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHY.

Warrington Society Have a Canal Trip.

MOUNTING SQUEEGEED GLOSSY PRINTS.

• The following (writes " in " Photo- graphy and Focas " for July 7) I have found to be the best method of mounting very glossy rints so that none whatever of the gloss will be lost, and the mounts will remain perfectly flat. The prints are squeegeed to the glass be- fore being trimmed. When almost dry water. proof sheets nearly the size cif the untrimmed prints are pasted down upon them and left until the paste is thoroughly dry. The prints are then stripped and trimmed. For mounting, hot glue not too thick is used in the ordinary way; a very little is brushed on the back of the print, taking care That none gets on the front, and the print is then laid on the mount, quickly rubbed down and put under gentle pressikre until the nexteay. It may seem more elaborate than some ofthe mounting methods used by the amateurs; but the results more thou justify the slight extra trouble. Next to dry-mounting, which is-not in everyone's reach, it is the best process I know.

WARRINGTON MONUMENTS.

2. Arpley Cannens.—Just as the difficulty of photographing O'Leary's statue is its height, so the cannons group in front of Arvid, Station cause trouble to the amateur by their length. They face practically north and south, and the most pleasing view-Point is from the north- east, with the big mass of the station fcr a back- ground. Looked at from the south side the,rail- ings and telegraph poles make an unpleasant distraction. To photograph them on a sunshiny day is therefore out of the question, and so a day when the clouds are plentiful bnt the light is bright is best chosen. Unfortunately, it is not a popular rendezvous for children, or the picture would be greatly enhanced in value by the introduction of a few figures of the little ones. The whole group is of itself a handsome monument, for which Warrington owes the late Sir Gilbert Greenall ample thanks.

W.P.S.-THE CANAL TRIP.

The Photographic- S-o.cie-t;'-s. trip; " .long the Trent and Mersey Canal under Mr. Walker's leadership has become an annual event. Ten members took part. They left Bank Quay at 1.30, and, changing at Acton Bridge, reached Northwich Station in about the same time that they could have done it on cycles.- The Quay at Broken Cross was chosen as the starting point and boarding the first barge going north they accomplished the journey to Acton Bridge in the reverse direction. With the exception of a mile through Anderton and the two tunnels the route is a very pretty one, and subjects for the camera were as usual plentiful along the banks.. The ladies were allowed to land at the mouths of the tunnels and walked over, but the gentlemen braved the terrors of the underworld, though they did no photography there. Teas were taken by the voyagers, and hot water was obtained aboard. After disembarking at Acton Bridge the party returned home by the 8.30 from the station, a mile away.

HISTORIC LANCASHIRE FAMILY.

By the death of the Earl of Ellesmere, which, we regret th state, took place on Monday morn-, ing at Bridgewater House, London, Lancashire has lost one of her most distinguished sons. The late Earl, who had attained the age of 66, was born in London in 1847, and was the third holder of the earldom, which was created in 1846. He succeeded to the title in 1862, and six years later married Lady Katharine Phipps, the sister of the present Marquis of NQI manby. The earldom was conferred in 1846 on the late nobleman's grandfather, who was a grand- nephew of the third and last Duke of Bridge- water, whose name was so prominently asso-. ciated with the inauguration of the canal sys- tem in England. The third Duke of Bridgewater was one of those who laid the foundations of Lancashire's present prosperity, for in 1759 he obtained an Act of Parliament under which he was empow- ered to construct a canal from Worsley to Man- chester. He retained Brindley, the famous engineer, to carry out his designs, and on July 17th, 1761, a boat laden with coal from Worsley safely negotiated the new waterway, and was unloaded at the Duke's wharf at Knott Mill. Later the canal was extended to Runcorn, and as the whole cost fell upon the Duke, he was forced to cut down his household expenses and to accept small loans from his friends. The canal was well patronised, passenger boats sail- •ing between Manchester and Worsley and Run- corn. Having finished the work he had set his heart upon, the Duke retired into private life, dividing his time between his London house and Worsley. He died in 1803, when his income from the canal si and mines totalled some 4;80,000 a year. -Eventually the first Earl of Ellesmere became the owner of both canals and mines. The canals were later disposed of, first to the Bridgewater Navigation Company, later to the Manchester Ship Canal.

The Duke, in 1803, shortly before his death, devised an elaborate will by which he turned his canals, collieries, and estates in Lancashire and Cheshire into a trust, known as the Bridge- water Trust, to be managed on behalf of his heirs during the lifetimes of several hundred persons, including every member of the House of Lords then living, and for 20 years afterwards. The last of these lives ended in 1883, and in 1903, just 100 years after the death of its founder, the trust came to an end and the estates passed into the hands of the late Earl. The Bridgewater Canal had been acquired by the Manchester Ship Cadal in 1887 at a price of ,t 1,710,000.

The Earl, who had literary and sporting tastes, had residences at Worsley Hall; Manches- ter; Stetchworth Park, Newmarket; Brackley, Northamptonshire; and Bridgewater House, London. He owned about 13,000 acres of land. He was a vice-president of the Lancashire County Cricket Club.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW EARL

••• •---- --- Lord Ellesmere leaves surviving four sons and five daughters. His eldest son, Viscount Brack- ley, who succeeds to the title, was born in 1872, and married in 1905 Violet, daughter of the Hon. F. W. Lambton, brother of Lord Durham, and has three daughters. Of Cle late peer's daughters, one is married to Lord Rochdale and another to Mr. Charles Hardy, of Chilharq Castle, Kent. Lady Rochdale, and her sisters, Lady Mabel and Lady Leila Egerton, are actively engaged in philanthropic work. The new peer bought a few years aqo from Lord Polwarth the beautiful estate of Mertoun, in Berwickshire, and has since made it his home.

LIFTING TULIPS.

In a letter to the Editor " H. 8." asks my -advice about lifting Tulips in a garden where the soil is heavy, and ale in one where it is light. I think there is no donbt,whatever that Tulips do better when they are lifted and re- planted every year, whatever the soil is. As the bulb loses the whole of its roots, or, to speak more correctly, as the new bulb makes no roots for a considerable time after the roots of the old one have perished, nothing is gained by their being left undisturbed, except perhaps an earlier start into life, which is just the one thing most of us do not want to facilitate. The foliage comes through the ground quite soon enough in our trying climate when the bulbs are planted in November. "11.13." also in- quires about the treatment of Tulips when they are lifted. As soon as the stems and leaves have gong quite withered-looking and dry, take up the bulbs, cut the dead foliage off, and spread out thinly in boxes or on a floor out of the reach of the sun and draughts, but with plenty of air. When they have become dry, clean the bulbs and store them in a dry place in paper bags until planting time. The smaller bulbils should be put by themselves in a nursery bed to grow on, and be planted in September. The very small ones had better be thrown away, except those of any very special sort—Joseph Jacob, in Line Gardino

WARRINGTON.

MEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

Rheumatism in Children.

Sometimes when a child complains of aches and pains in the bones and muscles it is set down to "growing pains," and the mistake only discovered when much damage has been done by the neglect of these ,symptoms. The growth of muscle and pone is 'absolutely painless. The so-called "growing pains" are invariably rheu- matic; and should be attefided to immediately. When a child complains of pains in the limbs, he must never be allowed to run about. A few clays' rest and care may ward off an attack of rheumatism or even rheumatic fever. Many of the complications from neglecting rheumatism are very serious. Heart disease and St. Vitus's dance not infrequently result from an attack of rheumatism.

Now to Treat.—lf a child suffers from joint l! and muscle pains, nervous twitchings, or sore throat; it is safe to say that he has a strong tendency to rheumatism, and when these occur 'great care must be taken to avoid chills. The diet is very important, and must consist of plenty of milk, light, easily digested food, with very little meat. The clothing next to the skin must be woollen, and, if the child gets over- heated, he must be well rubbed "down and re- dressed in dry clothes. If chilliness or fever are present give a hot bath, in which a lump of soda has been dissolved, dry well in front of a fire, and place between heated blankets, and give hot drinks of gruel or hot nilk. If this does no good send for a doctor.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
HINTS ON HEALTH.

Mumps and School Children.

Mumps is quite a common disease of child- hood, being highly infectious, and frequently occurring in 'epidemics. It has its seat in the salivary glands, of which there are three pairs— (I) the parotid glands, in front of the ear and at the angle of the jaw, (2) the sub-maxillary, right below each sid• of the lower jaw-bone, (3) the sub-lingual just below the chin. The characteristic deformity is due to the swelling of the parotids in front of the ears, ,although only one side may be affected. The disease is usually caught by contact with an affected per- son, but is liable to be carried by one not suffer- ing from the ailment, and the infection persist for a week or two after the swelling of the glands subsides.

How It is Spread. The swelling does not begin immediately after infection has taken place, for the latter re- mains dormant for two or three weeks during what is called the incubation period, before the glands on one side or another or on both sides simultaneously begin to swell. It is highly important that a child should remain in bed during the progress of the swelling, as inflam- mation of the more important internal organs is apt to take place, and if this occurs much pain and inflammation and prolonged convales- cence are sure to follow. The pain of mumps sometimes requires sedative treatment, hut in all cases the parts should be protected by cot- ton wool. Sometimes suppuration takes place, and in these cases the resulting abscess must be opened. Occasionally a parotid gland becomes inflamed, and an abscess arises due to poison other than that of the infectious disease called mumps, but this is unusual. In most cases the poison spreads from a diseased tooth.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
COOKERY CORNER. Some Useful Recipes.

Swiss milk toffee is delicious and can be made thus: Put a tablespoonful of cold water into a pan, and add two ounces of butter. Let it melt over the turned-down gas, and then add tablespoonful golden syrup and 11b. of castor sugar. Let all dissolve slowly; stir in a tin of Swiss milk, and boil twenty minutes without stirring. Lift from the stove, and add two teaspoonfuls of Vanilla.

Vegetable Marrow Jam. This recipe is. improved by the a.ddition of apples. Allow for 41b. vegetable marrow, 41b. of apples, 31b. white sugar, ounce ground ginger, 1 large lemon. Peel the marrow and remove the seeds; peel and core the apples, and cut both fruit rather small. Sprinkle the sugar over, and leave for 24 hours. Then boil for 20 minutes with the sugar. Pour into a crock, and leave till next day; boil up again with the ginger and grated rind and juice of the lemon. Con- tinue boiling until the jam is clear.

Mint Jelly.

Boil 111-i. granulated sugar in pint good brown malt vinegar for five minutes. Soften 2 ounces of gelatine in cold water, and then stir it into the vinegar over the gas till dis- solved. Season with salt and pepper, and add 6 or 7 ounces of fresh mint leaves chopped very fine. Stir all over the gas until the mixture begins to thicken; then turn it into glass jars to set firm.

A LESSON IN DISCIPLINE.

A Little Beene Between Willie and His Foolish Mother.

" Willie!" (No answer.) Willie!" "What?" "Stop pulling Ilie lace curtains." "But, ma, I ain't going to hurt them," "Let them alone, anyway." (Willie gives the curtains an extra hard tug.) "You contrary boy, do you want mamma to whip you?"

" Then don't put your hands on those curtain again." " Will if I want to."

_ "Why, what do you mean by talking to me like that? Go in the bedroom and shut the door. Go right in the bedroom, I say !" (Willie obeys and proceeds to kick the Is.int off the door panels.) "Listen to me, you naughty child. I certainly get the stick if you don't behave." (Willie kicks a splinter off the dressing table.) " Do you want to break mamma's heart?" Yes." "Why, Willie?" (Willie kicks his mother.)

"Papa, you will have to fetch the switch. It's on the clock shelf in the kitchen."

- --- --- (Papa brings the stick. Willie collapses on the floor, howling horribly.) "Hush, Willie! For goodness sake, don't make so much noise. What will the neighbours think?" (Willie howls as loud as he can)

" Listen, dearie, don't cry. Please don't cry. Mamma doesn't intend to whip you. No, in- deed, mamma wouldn't hurt her sweet boy for all the World. Stop, darling, poor little fright- ened fellow. There, there, now." "I'm hungry." " Bless his heart, he says he is hungry. What shall mamma get for you, dear? Some bread and butter with a little honey?" "No; I want some chocolates." "We haven't any, Willie. You ate the last this afternoon. Won't an orange do?" "How about a nice dish of pineapple with a big piece of sponge cake?" , " I hate pineapple." " Wouldn't a nice thick piece of mince pie taste good?" "I say I want chocolates!"

WALKER'S BIG TRIP.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
A Glorion Day Sp:nt at Blackpool.

THREE SPECIAL TRAIN-LOADS.

More so than in xi:tally other towns the good feeling which invariably exists between em- ployers and employees is manifested in War- rington on various occasions, and at least once a year the masters and men of most works leave the whirr and turmoil of everyday occupation , and seek relaxation by the sea or in the country. 1 One of the most popular trips of this char- acter is that organised by Messrs. Peter Walker and Son,•Ltd., and this year's outing to Black- pool on Saturday proved to be one of the most enjoyable ever held. No more suitable plane could be chosen than Blackpool, which increases in popularity every year. This is the third time in succession that Walkers', Warring- ton," have patronised this "Brighton of the North," and 'again glorious weather favoured the trippers. Every accommodation was pro- vided for the company, and three well-filled special trains left Bank Quay about 6 a.m., arriving at Blackpool in good time. With a whole 'day before them the visitors soon com- menced to enjoy themsel+es and participate in the concessions made by the firm, which included free admission to the Winter Gardens, North Pier and Pavilion, Tower entertainments, one ascent of the Tewer, and a free trip to Fleet- wood and back, the latter being taken advantage of by an exceedingly large majority. In addition to these and other usual allow- ances to the workmen each man received a double railway ticket, and specially reduced fares were granted to friends desirous of joining the excursion. The sun shone beautifully all day, and Warringtonians were to be seen in almost every part of the town, particularly on South Shore, that miniature Coney Island, crammed full of every kind of diverting enter- tainments, such as the joy wheel, figure eight, water chute, and numerous side shows, all tend- ing to enjoyment of the heartiest kind. The Tower and Winter Gardens were invaded at night, and the return journey to Warrington was made about 10 o'clock, everyone being unanimous in their opinion as to the excellent arrangements made. There was a large attendance of members of the staff and invited guests at the dinner in the Hotel Metropole just prior to the departure for home, Mr. J. Fulton-Smith presiding. The toast of the King having been enthusi- astically drunk on the proposition of the Chair- man,

Mr. W. A. Wooding (Midland Railway Com- pany) submitted "Health and Prosperity to Messrs Peter Walker and Son." He was sure all the visitors had spent the day in a very happy manner, and he hoped they would all have the pleasure of meeting again next year under equally pleasant circumstances. (Hear, hear.)

The toast was drunk with musical honours.

Major Potter, responding on behalf of the firm, said it was a pleasure to him to hear the firm so well spoken of, and Mr. Fulton-Smith and he were pleased to welcome the guests. (Applause.)

Old Harrison (L. and N.-W. Railway Company) alluded to the large number of excur- sion trains which had poured into the town that day, and said those present would easily realise that to cope with the huge traffic the railway organisation had to be of an elaborate and carefully-organised character. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Brown (Great Central) ' thanked Messrs. Peter Walker and Son for their hospitality. Alderman Porshaw, who was received with applause, proposing the health of the chairman, said that he and the other guests had been treated in princely fashion. It should be re- membered that Warrington was the birthplace of the firm, and the town ought to be proud of the fact and give it more consideration than it had done hitherto. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Messrs. Walker believed in &the old and true maxim that a labourer was worthy of his hire. The officials and workmen were treated with the greatest possible consideration', and in these cir- cumstances it was no wonder that the firm pros- pered. Mr. Pulton-Smith was at one time a colleague of his on the Town Council. He was sorry he was not still a member, as there was a need for the exercise there of those abilities he was known to possess. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.)

The toast was received with musical honours, and cheers were given for Mrs. Fulton-Smith. The Chairman, in reply, said he had no idea the toast was to be submitted, as they had endeavoured to avoid the making of speeches on these occasions, but he could not fail to respond to a toast which had been so heartily received. It was a great pleasure to attend these outings, which were made possible by the generosity of the Chairman and managing directors of the firm. The annual excursion was always regarded as a red-letter day for everyone in Warrington associated with the firm. It brought them all .together, and helped to produce that spirit of good-feeling which should animate every big con- cern. They were out to conquer the world with regard to their beer. There was no doubt the firm brewed the best beer, and those who did not appreciate it did not know what really good beer was. (Laughter and applause.) He re- gretted the unavoidable absence of Mr. Hutton and Mr. Parry. Some one, he explained, had to remain at home while others were enjoying themselves. (Hear, hear.)

THE EEPRESS WRECK.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
Due to the Storstad Changing Course in a Fog.

The Royal Commission .appointed to inquire into the loss of the Empress of Ireland gave their decision at Quebec on Saturday and found that the Storstad was to blame.

The Court held that the collision was solely due to the fact that the Storstad ported her helm during the fog, thus directly coming across the track of the Empress of Ireland. Captain Kendall, of the Empress of Ireland, was exoner- ated from all blame for his navigation of the liner, although the Court held that it might have been wiser to give the Storstad a wider, berth. The fact that the engines of the Em- press of Ireland were reversed was in no way contributory to the disaster. The decision was read in the Court House where the Commission ,held its last meeting. Lord Mersey himself read" the findings-

After giving a summary of the account of the disaster as supplied by Captain Kendall and the first officer of the Empress of Ireland and by Mr. Tuftenes, who was in charge of the Stor- stad at the time Of the collision, and the other officers who were on the bridge with him, the findings proceeded to show that Mr. Tuftenes was to blame for the accident, because the collier's course was Changed. The part of The report dealing with the responsibility for the disaster says:—

"We regret to have to impute the blame to anyone in connection with this lamentable disaster. We should not do so if we felt that any reasonable alternative was left to us. We can, however, come to no other conclusion than that Mr. Tuftenes was wrong and negligent in altering his course in the fog, as he undoubtedly did, and that he was wrong and negligent in keeping the navigation of the vessel in his own hands and failing to call the captain wliet‘ he saw the'fog coming on. "It is not to be supposed that this disaster was in any way attributable to any special characteristics of the St. Lawrence waterway. It was a disaster which might have occurred in the Thames, Clyde, or Mersey, or elsewhere in similar circumstances." .

• ---Th-e--;7Tnit-e-s-7;—C--a-il-a-d-lan correspondent says that the findings of tho Court were not wholly unexpected, and great satisfaction is expressed that the St. Lawrence route is not held respon- sible for the accident. The Mansion House Fund for the widows, orphans, and relatives of those who were lost in the Empress of Ireland on Monday Was £43,561, and the Liverpool Fund £25,000. Canadian contributions to the extent of £20,000 have, in addition, been made. It is estimated that about £BO.OOO more will be required to deal adequately with the eases of the sufferers.

THE CANTERBURY BELL.

This is one of the gayest of our flowers at the present time, and is a plant for all gardens, whether large or small, for it may be used with good effect as a.border plant, in a large bed, or as a pot plant. lii all cases it is very decora- tive. If the old flowers are continually picked off, the plants will remain in bloom for a much longer period than they would do other- wise. When the plants are over, their place may be filled with summer-flowering Chrysan- themums, and a gap thus prevented. If seed of Canterbury Bells for next year's display has not already been soivn, it should he put in without delay.—" The Garden."

CRICKET,

IVARRINGTON WIN.

Local Club Matches.

The Warrington senior eleven created a sur- prise at Stockport on Saturday, for with only three wickets down they passed the home team's score. Stockport made the commendable aggre- gate of 222, the chief contributor to which was S. R. V. Stockdale, who had compiled 66 before he was stumped by Williamson. W. L. Minett was bowled by B. Silcock when three runs short of the half-century, whilst G. A. Baker's score reached .27 before he was given marching orders. F. IV. Archer's total had reached 24 when the home team's innings was brought to a close. Extras were responsible for no fewer than 18 runs. For the visitors W. Richardson with- stood the attack to the end, making 76 runs, but the highest score of the day was made from the bat of T. Wright, who was caught when he had made exactly half the number compiled by the home team—namel,y, 111. G. Woodcock made 20 before he was stumped, but Hannam was not allowed to reach double figures, being bowled by Archer. The only other player who paid a visit to the crease was H. Hawthorn, who had scored two and kept his wicket intact. Extras accounted for six runs, bringing the Warrington total to 224. For the visitors W. Bennett (three wickets for 46 runs) and B. Silcock (four for 63) were the most successful bowlers. T.- e wicket which was captured by Hannam cost him 57 runs.

The Warrington Conservatives were opposed to Divez at Arpley on Saturday, and proved themselves the winners, scoring 103, against their opponents' 23. The highest score of the day was 31, made by Heywood, who defended his wicket until the end; whilst Hunter secured 30 before he was caught. There were seven non- scorers on the Dives side, in addition to one who failed to put in an appe*rtee.

Padgate Wesleyans were 'hopelessly defeated at the hands of the Working Men's Mission at Arpley on Saturday, for they conld only muster. an aggregate of eight, five of which were secured by G. Ball. The Mission, with a wicket in hand, compiled 124, T. McGarr, who made 27 without losing his wicket, • being the highest scorer. S. Lloyd was responsible for 25 runs; W. Appleton and "Mr. Extras" equally divid- ing 36 runs. Double-figure totals were also made by H. Oakley (16) and T. O'Niel (15). J. McGarr was also successful with the ball, for his two wickets were obtained at a cost of only a single run, whilst S. Lloyd's six wickets were respon- sible for one run each.

Orford Juniors failed to obtain the mastery over Widnes St. Ambrose at the chemical town on Saturday. The home team's effort reached 30, and Orford, in their reply, fell six short of that number. J. Ashton, for the visitors, ob- tained 13—the highest score of the day—and J. Reardon seven runs. Six of the other Juniors were unable to credit themselves with a single.

Grappenhall entertained Weaverham and ran out easy winners—in fact they doubled the visi- tors' score. The premier score of the match was the 24 compiled by A. E. Evans, of the home team, J. Ford coming next with a single less. They, together with W. Watson, were the chief contributors towards a total of 98. C. Buckley, for the visitors was 15 not out; he was the only double-figure man on his side.

Emmanuel subdued Newton Congregational on Saturday, the Newton team being all out for 13. For the winners Albert Hughes distinguish.,d himself with both bat and ball, heading the scores with 13, and taking eight wicket i for seven runs. He also accomplished the "hat trick." A. Oakes also batted well, and kept his Wicket intact, with nine to his credit. For the losers F. Guttridge wa's the only one to make a stand, making seven of the 13 runs.

- Bold-street Wesleyans paid the penalty of putting a weak team out on Saturday by sus- taining their first reverse of the seasou, at the hands of Penketh Friends' School. 1.. w. Weaver, with 23 to his credit, was the only player on the Wesleyans' side to reach double figures, the innings closing for 13. I 'en k etli started disastrously, seven wickets teing do 11 for 13, but Baker hit out well and the scum WEI 9 he carried to 39 for nine wickets innings would have before left. closedßut fforta thisdropped u figure, catch u tfi thepr tg by the mis- take the last two men carried the score to 52, thus 'enabling Penketh to win by 12 runs. For the Wesleyans, W. Stringer again distinguished himself by performing the "hat trick," and, taking nine wickets for 17 runs.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Another of those excellent programmes to which we are getting accustomed is presented at the Hippodrome this week. The Yamagata Japanese Troupe pay their first visit and per- form the most marvellous acrobatic feats in daring and clever fashion. There are many new features introduced, and the performance by the diminutive youngest member of the troupe is very smart, and the cause of much laughter. Edna Lyall, described as the York- shire Nightingale, sings very sweetly, and fully merits her title in well-chosen numbers. Richard Wally, assisted by La Belle Jessica, gives a novel juggling turn, the chief item being that in which he plays the part of the human bil- liard table. With pockets on various parts of his clothing, he juggles with several billiard balls and each drop in one or other of the pockets. Mina Bax is a very artistic danseuse, who pleases with every item. The Leotard Brothers cause much laughter as the comical clowns; and Stacey and Williams also please the audience as successful entertainers in a draw- ing-room interlude. Harry Levaine appears as a dame comedian, and several of his gags and witticisms are smart.

Next Week's Programme. The programme for nest week includes : Abomah, the African Ragtime Singing Giantess; Charles Grantley, erratic comedian; Bros. Brad- shaw, acrobatic eccentrics; Arthur Page, Lan- cashire dialect comedian; Henry Helme, French Alpine vocalist; Huley and Bent, comedy act; Valvero, in scena, "A Naval Bombardment at Night"; and the customary Perfection pictures.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"Good-bye, Sweetheart, Good-bye.."

A • popular domestic drama is attracting patrons tqr the Royal Court Theatre this week. " Good-bye, Sweetheart, Good-bye" is full of sensational incidents, and a rather sordid story which, however, has a happy ending, is illus- trated in a striking fashion. Mr. Horace Stanley (the author of the piece) plays the part of the genial Bill Burnstead in a pleasing style, re- lieving the heavier aspects of the play with a good supply of rich humour. Miss Maggie English makes a sympathetic appeal as Fay Garth, the victimised sufferer. As the breezy and sympathetic Jack Fortescue Mr. Harry Scott is a decided success, and Mr. Oswald Douglas gives a clever impersonation of Stephen Hawke, a calculating scoundrel. Miss Chrissie Dunbar is also well suited to the part of Jess Hawke.

ANDREWS' PICTURE PALACE. A , strong blending of tragedy, humour and _pathos abounds in the great film, "The World, the Flesh, and the Devil," which will form the principal attraction at Andrews', Bridge Foot Theatre, on Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday next. The film, a very long one, is a pictorial version of the drama produced by Lawrence Cowan, four years ago, and abounds with exciting incidents, which thrill and please. The melodramatic characteristics of the original story have been wcil preserved, in fact in several instances they have neen accentuated, 14nd all who pay a visit to this-house of entertainment will not regret it. Not for a single moment is excitement allowed to wane, for it grips the audience imme- diately the title is thrown on the screen, and does not release them until the film has told its full story. It is pleasing to notice that the management, despite the extra expenditure they have gone to, in order to bring the picture to Warrington, are not to raise the prices of ad- mission, an act which will give great satisfac- tion. Advance bookings can be made at Pear- son's, Bridge-street, as well as at Andrews' Theatre.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
WATER POLO.

%%ARRINGTON'S BUSY WEEK.

The Cup Gone.

The Warrington team have played three matches within the course of a week, but they have been unfortunate enough to lose two of them, one being in the second round for the Lancashire Junior Cup, at Accrington. DEFEAT OF THE "LINNETS."

They were successful in beating Runcorn away, this being the " Linnets' " first defeat at home. Runcorn defended the deep end, and from the swim-off it was evident that the game was going to be very keen. Beaman secured the ball for Runcorn and sent well up the bath, only to be repulsed by S. lace, who worked a grand opening for H. Sisson, but the latter, how- ever, missed. Turner gave to S. Ince, who scored easily. This livened the home team up a little, but from a pass by S. Roberts, C. Turner scored from near the half-way line, giving the home custodian no chance. Half-time arrived with the score 2-0 in favour of the visitors. Soon after the re-start, S. Inc added another goal from a pass by C. Turner, and H. Sisson put the Warringtonians further ahead after dribbling the ball nearly half the length of the bath. Result: Warrington, four goals; Run- corn, nil.

, In the squadron race Warrington won by five yards, after haring been well behind. FAREWELL TO THE CUP.

In the match at Accrington on Monday War- rington defended the deep end and were early on the aggressive, Sisson and lace sending in good shots. The home team scored through Haworth, Woods having no chance. Half-time arrived with Accrington leading by one goal to nil.

Immediately on re-starting Warrington equal- ised through Sisson. Westall scored two more goals for the home team, but Sisson added an- other for Warrington from a pass by C. Turner. Westall added the fourth goal for Accrington. Woods, in goal, played well for Warrington. Accrington won by four goals to two, after one of the best exhibitions of polo played at Accring- ton, both teams having played the game in true sportsmanlike fashion. BEATEN AT HOME.

Leigh were entertained in the local baths on Thursday evening, and beat the homesters by four, goals to three, after a very spirited game. In the first half Warrington were by far the superior team, and, defending the shallow end they were successful in breaking up the combin- ation of the Leigh forwards. On many occa- sions the forwards on both sides had hard lines, the ball hitting the bar and uprights whenever a shot was sent in. After a few minutes' play Turner opened Warrington's account with a splendid shot which gave the Leigh custodian no chance. The same player only just failed to score shortly afterwards. Just before the

interval Pepper added Warrington's second goal, the visitors not having beaten L. Graham, the Warrington goalie. In the second half Leigh were the aggressors, and a feature of this part of the game was the swift and accurate back-hooking of their tricky forward, Carlton, who soon equalised the scores with two well-placed shots. Warrington tried hard to force their way, but *ere repulsed al- most every time, although several shots, well placed and sent in in stinging fashion, were cleverly saved by the Leigh goalkeeper. Carl- ton added another couple of goals, one coming from a good drive in the middle of the bath. Fuller, the new Warrington recruit and cham- pion of Ithyl, had to leave the water owing to a severe attack of cramp, this being attributed to the change from sea water. Thus handicapped Warrington had very little chance, but they played determinedly, and Turner added their third goal just before the call of time.

OTHER ATTRACTIONS. In a squadron race Leigh also won by half a length.

WORKS' COMPETITION.

The second round of the "Sir Gilbert Greenall" Works' Cup Competition was decided, when Crosfield's junior team defeated Whitecross after a close contest.

EXHIBITION.

This was followed by a clever exhibition of ornamental swimming by Miss Warrington, of Manchester, the youngest female high diver.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON ADULT SCHOOLS.

The Question of Environment.

At Stockton Heath Men's Class on Sunday Mr. Arthur Spenser, a member of the school, gave a lecturette on "Environment." At the commencement the speaker said owing to the _vast amount of ground which could be covered if he were to attempt to go fully into his sub- ject he could only in the time' at his disposal deal with a very small section'bf it. He spoke from the standpoints of biology', evolution, and economics, and gave an interesting account under each of these sections. A good discussion followed. Mr. Greenwood opened the discussion on the Bible lesson for the day on the disobedi- ence of the Israelites.

===== 0002601_19140718_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
SANKEY BRIDGES MEN'S.

On Sunday morning Mr. Charles Wilcock - son gave the second part of his lecture on "The Apparent Errors in the Bible." The speaker said that in the past the question of the inspira- tion of the Bible was not approached in a proper manner. The teaching that the Bible had been written by the finger of God and was not to be questioned was not satisfactory. The result of such teaching was seen in the attacks made upon the book by sceptics and unbelievers.

.. - The true value could only be appreciated by bringing intelligence and common sense into its sttlbly. Turning to the question of mistakes the speaker said that some of the statements made in the various parts required a good deal of unbiassed inquiry in order to get at their real meaning.

Mr. iVilcockson then took a number of pas- sages from the Old Testament which have been the subject of considerable controversy, and proceeded to show that the apparent errors were due either to want of scientific knowledge on the part. of the compiler or to the manner in which the Oriental described events, the Western mind taking as literal statements which were merely figurative expressions in explanation of natural phenomena.

An interesting discussion followed the address, the chairman in summing up pointing out that the fact that what had once, for want of precise knowledge, been looked upon as super-natural, and could now be explained by natural means, did not militate against the Bible or decrease its value. The fecognition of the human tend- ency to make mistakes, even in saored writings, rather strengthened the case than weakened it.

Appointments by Public Authorities. WARRINGTON UNION. • CLERK AT COTTAGE HOMES. Applications are invited for the position of Superintendent's Clerk at the Cottage Homes, Padgate, near Warrington. Applicants must be well educated and quick at figures, and must not be under the age of 16 rears. Salary 1'26 per annum, together with ilinner and tea daily (non-resident). List of duties may be obtained from the Superintendent of the schools. Applications must be made upon forms, to be obtained from me, the undersigned, by forward- ing a stamped-addressed foolscap envelope, and the same must be returned duly completed not later than Monday, the 27th July, 1914. Canvassing, either directly or indirectly, will di squ alify. By order, ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY, Clerk. Union Offices, Bewsey-chambers, Warrington, 17th July, 1914.

COUNTY OF LANCASTER. REGISTER OF COUNTY VOTERS. The Finance Committee of the County Council of the County Palatine of Lancaster are prepared to receive TENDERS from Printers having their place of Business within the County of Lancaster, for PRINTING the REGISTER OF VOTERS for the Parliamentary Divisions of the County of Lancaster, to come into force on the Ist of January, 1915. Speecifications of the conditions and full par- ticulars may be obtained on application to the Clerk of the County Council, Preston. The conditions provide that the contractor whose Tender is accepted shall pay the standard rate of wages and observe the recognised hours and conditions of labour in his district. Tenders to be sent, addressed, under seal, to the Chairman of the Finance Committee, County Offices, Preston, on or before Saturday, the fitli day of August, 1914. The Committee do not bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. HARCOURT E. CLARE, Clerk of the County Council. Preston, 17th July, 1914.

The first round is to be played as early as can be arranged in, November It was also decided to Offer the "Starkey" Cup as a knock-out competition, open to all the clubs in the Warrington and District League area, the first round to be played the second Saturday in January, 1915. It was agreed that both divisions of the League should be open age competitions, and this decision necessitated the alterations of sev- eral rules, which were keenly discussed and passed. The Honorary Secretary informed the repre- sentatives that the registration forum for the signing on of players could be had on applica- tion after the first of August, and that the con- ference meeting of club secretaries would be held on the 12th of August for the arrangements of futures for the coming season. A ROW THAT LASTED A WEEK. Strange Affair at St. Helens. It was stated at St. Helens Police Court on Monday that a row between neighbours there had been in progress for a week. As a result of the prolonged fight ten members of the fami- lies of Paton and Graham were brought before the magistrates, charged with a breach of the peace. Constable Johnson said that the parties lived next door to one another, and they had con- verted the district into a perfect bedlam. On the 7th inst. they were fighting with broomsticks and other articles in the middle of Boundary- road:- Witness and Constable Toft drove them into the house, and they were occupied for about two hours clearing the crowd away. After they had been stopped fighting in front the crowd made a rush round to the back of the houses, where the battle had been resumed in the back- yards. Eight of the accused were women, and some of them were4unning up and down the road with their hair streaming, swearing, Screaming, and scratching like maniacs. Constable Connors said every night for about a week these people were fighting. On the Tuesday night Boundary-road was blocked for over an hour by a crowd of about 1,000 people. Several ladies had to go round by Dunriding- lane in order to get toPrescot-road. He called on two other officers, and they made a hurried detour by Elliott-street in order to succour the officers who were in the fight. Defendants upbraided one another in Court, and by their talk about flying saucepans, kettles and jam-jars, amply corroborated the evidence of police as to the serious character of the fighting. The Chairman (Alderman Martin) said re- spectable people could not live in the neighbour- hood, and that sort of pandemonium must be stopped. Each defendant must find sureties of ..t5 and two of £2 10s. to be of good behaviour for twelve months or serve 28 days each.

INCIDENTS IN BOWLERS' WORLD.

ANGLING. Warrington Workingmen's Associa- tion. N.A. CENTRE. • POOR HOLIDAY SPORT. Several bream have been weighed in at the headquarters this last week from Chester way, but the holidays have been a great disappoint- ment to many who at considerable expense went in search of specimens in the hope of bringing home something at least to put on the scales. Several who visited Pool Quay caught fish galore, but none to put on the scales; others who visited Melveley shared the same fate. Sport could not be said to be entirely off, but "class " was. This week end most of the members were with the Bay -Horse Club fishing their match at Aintree. This venture has proved quite success- ful, and the centre members will again visit the spot for their match on the 26th inst. 1 They have also decided to go by rail and have engaged saloon accommodation also. Train fare, including saloon, 2s. 3d. Depart from Central ' Station 9.30 a.m. RIVER WEAVER. As far as fishing matches in this water are concerned, members may take it for granted that the Warrington N.A. cannot arrange any matches there this season. We have not come to any terms up to the present, and the mem- bers themselves are arranging to take their contests elsewhere, for this season at any rate. The Bay Horse Club members are quite satis- fied, and from comments that I have heard since their match I take it that the members will lose nothing by finding "fresh waters " other than Northwich way. THE GOWY. This favoured stream had not the usual num- ber of visitors owing to the Bay Horse match, but we heard one or two members had fair catches, but not heavy enough to pass over the scales, the weather marring any possible good sport in this stream. CHESTER. A number of Warrington anglers visited the river and the canal this week, and from infor- mation that has come to hand these Members had plenty of sport, particularly in the canal amongst the roach and eels. NORTON CANAL. Members who have visited this canal during the week have had the best sport, a large num- ber of roach being hooked. They had to be re- turned owing to their size, but a good few of the members are determined to visit this canal regu- larly, knowing that there are specimen fish. Owing to the enormous traffic that goes con- tinually through during the week the larger fish are getting moved from one place to another, making it very difficult to locate the spot where the fish are feeding. NOTICE. Members who are in the prize list at the Bay Horse Club will have it made known to them and also when the prizes are likely to be given out by attending the Bay Horse clubroom this (Saturday) or Monday evening. Opening match of headquarters .(Gladstope Arms), July 26th. There will be a splendid 1 , array of prizes. All members must be in benefit to take part in the contest. A.S. ON THE WING.
